Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-ai-facilitation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are significant issues with using the Cannon cluster for web crawling/scraping, even for academic research. **FASRC's official policy strongly discourages it.** From the [Web Scraping Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy/): > We *highly discourage* using the cluster itself to scrape data. Due to its size and ease of parallelization of processes, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers. **Please seek another avenue for data acquisition first.** **Key requirements before you proceed:** 1. **Contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity** using the cluster. 2. It is *highly preferable* to scrape the data **elsewhere** (e.g., your own machine, a cloud instance, or a dedicated scraping service) and then **bring the data to Cannon** for processing. 3. If the data is sensitive, confidential, or contains human subjects, compliance review is **required** before placing it on the cluster. 4. Academic use does **not** exempt you from the target site's terms of service, robots.txt, or other usage policies. **Why this matters:** Spinning up hundreds of cores to parallelize web requests can easily overwhelm target servers, leading to IP blacklisting, legal action, or disruption to other Harvard researchers sharing the same network infrastructure. **Recommended approach:** - Scrape the data on a single node or external service first - Once you have the dataset, transfer it to Cannon and use the cluster for the actual analysis/computation If you'd like help with the next steps (e.g., transferring data to the cluster, or optimizing your analysis code for Cannon), let me know.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.

Due to its size and ease of parallelization of processes, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers. **Please seek another avenue for data acquisition first.** **Key requirements before you proceed:** 1. **Contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity** using the cluster. 2. Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/academic-use

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, **your account is not misconfigured** — this is expected behavior. Here's what's happening: ### Home directories are not available in Globus Home directories are intentionally **not shared via Globus** under any circumstances. This is a security measure to prevent accidental sharing of authentication keys or passwords. You cannot browse into your home directory through Globus. ### The "Everyone" folder is not available in Globus The `Everyone` directory is **not available through Globus**. Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` and `Users/$USER` (though `Users` has been deprecated as of 2025). The `Everyone` and `Transfer` directories are intended for internal sharing on the HPC cluster and are not exposed via Globus. ### What you *can* see in Globus When using the "Harvard FAS RC Holyoke" collection, you should only see: - Your `Lab` directory and its subdirectories - Any folders inside `Lab` (which are visible to the whole lab by default) If your lab share itself is not appearing in Globus, or if expected subdirectories within `Lab` are missing, you should contact FASRC support. Otherwise, this behavior is working as designed. For more details, see the [Globus File Transfer documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/).
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.

❓ Question

I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
1) **Summary of Attempt**: The agent attempted to retrieve specific documentation regarding the optimal method for packaging large numbers of small files for Globus transfers by querying the vector store multiple times with variations of the user's request. 2) **Best Possible Answer**: For transferring a directory with roughly half a million small files via Globus, the recommended approach is to **archive the directory into a single compressed file (e.g., using `tar` and `gzip`)** before initiating the transfer. Transferring hundreds of thousands of individual small files is inefficient and prone to errors due to high overhead; bundling them into a single tarball significantly reduces transfer time and metadata processing. You can create the archive on the cluster (e.g., `tar -czf output_files.tar.gz /path/to/directory`) and then transfer the resulting `.tar.gz` file via Globus. 3) **Note**: This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
Tar the directory into subset files first. FASRC recommends that your tar files range in size from 1-100 GiB — that size range transfers more quickly (especially if the transfer is interrupted) and is smaller to retrieve if it ever needs to come back from Tape. If you leave hundreds or thousands of files in a single directory, Globus will go into an 'endpoint is too busy' state and your job will timeout, restart, timeout, etc. FASRC also recommends reading their 'Tips for using tar to archive data' page before tarring.

Question 47: question_47

❓ Question

I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/7zip

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most likely reason your checksums don't match is that **the directory contents changed between when you ran the initial `shasum` and when you created the tar archive**. As noted in FASRC's [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/): > If the data in the directory is modified after you've run the checksum and before you've tar'd it, then the checksum will no longer match later when un-tar and compare later. If you need to tar an active filesystem, then checksum'ing will not be useful to you. ### What to do instead 1. **Checksum *after* archiving** — Generate the checksum of the tar file itself, not the directory contents: ```bash shasum myarchive.tar.gz > myarchive.tar.gz.shasum ``` Then verify after extraction: ```bash shasum -c myarchive.tar.gz.shasum ``` 2. **If you must verify individual files**, run the checksum *after* extraction and compare against a checksum file created at the same time as the tar (not before): ```bash # When creating the archive: find myscans/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > checksums.shasum tar czf myarchive.tar.gz myscans/ # After extraction: find myscans/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort | diff - checksums.shasum ``` 3. **For active directories**, consider using a tool like `rsync` with checksums or a version-control approach instead, since the data may change during the archiving process.
✅ Expected Answer
The checksum depends on the path you hand to find, so the same files hashed two different ways produce two different values. FASRC's guidance is to always do it the exact same way: cd to the directory *above* the one you are archiving (e.g. /n/mypath/scans) and then run find against the relative directory name (myscans) — not the full path. Two other things will break the comparison: (1) modifying the data after you took the checksum but before you tarred it (a checksum is useless on an active filesystem), and (2) storing the checksum file itself inside the tar, since its presence changes the checksum unless you remove it after un-tarring and before re-running.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Question 48: question_48

❓ Question

RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the archive is not corrupt. This is expected behavior when extracting tar files created by Starfish. When Starfish creates a tar archive, it captures the **entire absolute path** of the files inside the archive. So when you extract it, tar recreates that full path relative to your current working directory. For example, if you tarred `/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX` using Starfish and then extracted it in `/n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/`, you'll end up with: ``` /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX ``` This is why you see a nested tree with `ProjectX` buried at the bottom. **To fix this**, you can either: 1. **Extract to the root** (use caution, as this will recreate the full path on your filesystem): ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar -C / ``` 2. **Extract to a temporary location and then move just the `ProjectX` folder** to where you want it: ```bash mkdir temp_extract tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar -C temp_extract mv temp_extract/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX /desired/location/ rm -rf temp_extract ``` 3. **Use `--strip-components`** to remove the leading directories from the path during extraction: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar --strip-components=3 -C /desired/location/ ``` (Adjust the number based on how many directory levels you want to strip.) For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving).
✅ Expected Answer
The archive is fine. Starfish tars in place and captures the entire path inside the tar file, so extracting recreates that whole path underneath your output directory. Extracting a Starfish tar of /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX into /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp gives you /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX. Move the ProjectX directory to where you actually want it, then remove the leftover empty n/holylabs/jharvard_lab tree. Use plain `rm` rather than `rm -rf`, so it errors out if other files remain or you typed the wrong path.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving
💬 Agent Messages (2)

Question 49: question_49

❓ Question

I already pushed a bunch of archives into our lab's tape area on Globus and then realized I named them badly. Can I just rename them, or move a couple into a different folder to tidy things up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Short answer: **you can rename and move files/folders in the tape staging area via Globus**, but once the data has migrated to tape, those operations are no longer possible. Here's what you need to know: ### If the data is still in the disk-based staging area The tape staging area is a disk-based buffer before migration to tape. You can use the Globus File Manager to: - **Rename** files and folders - **Move** files into different folders within your lab's tape collection This is the same as managing files on any normal filesystem through Globus. ### If the data has already migrated to tape Once data has been migrated to tape, you **cannot** rename or move files through Globus. The tape filesystem is not owned by FASRC, and there is no way to modify file names or directory structure on tape itself. You would need to: 1. Retrieve the data from tape back to the staging area (if retrieval is possible) 2. Rename/move the files in the staging area 3. Re-transfer them to tape ### Important notes - Read/write access to the tape collection applies to the **entire lab collection** — you can't restrict permissions on individual folders ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)) - The underlying tape filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC staff cannot set permissions or modify files on your behalf ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)) **Recommendation:** Check whether your data is still in the staging area or has already migrated to tape. If it's still in staging, go ahead and rename/move through Globus. If it's already on tape, you'll need to retrieve it first. Need help checking the status of your data or retrieving from tape?
✅ Expected Answer
No — do not delete, move, or rename data once it has been transferred to the tape buffer. The Globus buffer gives you write access to upload data destined for tape, and all changes within your lab's Globus collection are automatically picked up by the tape robot. Deletions on the Globus buffer may result in permanent deletion on tape, and deleting files that have already been placed on tape removes the 'link' and makes retrieval impossible. Mass deletions, moves, and file renaming require cumbersome manual intervention. Name files appropriately before you transfer them, and work with your PI or lab data manager on your lab's tape storage protocols.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Question 50: question_50

❓ Question

Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Globus **does not support** sharing a single subdirectory of a collection. Globus shares are scoped to an entire endpoint or a single collection — you cannot restrict a share to just one folder within a larger collection. Here are the practical options: 1. **Create a separate endpoint for the project folder.** If the project folder lives on a filesystem that can be mounted as its own endpoint (e.g., a separate mount point, a container, or a dedicated storage path), you can create a Globus endpoint for just that path and share it. This is the cleanest approach but requires the folder to be independently mountable. 2. **Share the entire collection and rely on OS-level permissions.** Create a Globus share for the full collection, but set the underlying filesystem permissions (e.g., Unix permissions or ACLs) so the postdoc can only access the one project folder. The postdoc would see the full collection in Globus but would only be able to read/write the permitted folder. 3. **Use a Globus endpoint with a chroot or container.** If you can set up a chroot environment or container that exposes only the desired folder, you can create a Globus endpoint for that chroot and share it. For more details on creating and managing shares, see [Creating and Managing Shares](https://docs.globus.org/api/share_managers/create_and_manage_shares/). If you need help setting up a separate endpoint for a specific folder, let me know more about how that folder is stored (filesystem, mount point, etc.) and I can suggest a more specific approach.
✅ Expected Answer
You cannot. There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus. The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC cannot set those permissions; everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system. (The PI and General Manager can add lab members to the collection, but only to all of it.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access
💬 Agent Messages (6)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Globus share specific folder subdirectory permissions postdoc
🛠️ Tool Call #3: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🛠️ Tool Call #4: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🛠️ Tool Call #5: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Globus share specific subdirectory folder permissions postdoc
🤖 Assistant Message #6
Globus **does not support** sharing a single subdirectory of a collection. Globus shares are scoped to an entire endpoint or a single collection — you cannot restrict a share to just one folder within a larger collection. Here are the practical options: 1. **Create a separate endpoint for the project folder.** If the project folder lives on a filesystem that can be mounted as its own endpoint (e.g., a separate mount point, a container, or a dedicated storage path), you can create a Globus endpoint for just that path and share it. This is the cleanest approach but requires the folder to be independently mountable. 2. **Share the entire collection and rely on OS-level permissions.** Create a Globus share for the full collection, but set the underlying filesystem permissions (e.g., Unix permissions or ACLs) so the postdoc can only access the one project folder. The postdoc would see the full collection in Globus but would only be able to read/write the permitted folder. 3. **Use a Globus endpoint with a chroot or container.** If you can set up a chroot environment or container that exposes only the desired folder, you can create a Globus endpoint for that chroot and share it. For more details on creating and managing shares, see [Creating and Managing Shares](https://docs.globus.org/api/share_managers/create_and_manage_shares/). If you need help setting up a separate endpoint for a specific folder, let me know more about how that folder is stored (filesystem, mount point, etc.) and I can suggest a more specific approach.

Question 53: question_53

❓ Question

A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/network.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you download data from a FASSE compute node, the remote server will see the IP address of Harvard’s outbound proxy, **`rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu`** (port 3128), not your individual compute node’s IP. This is because all outbound internet traffic from FASSE goes through this proxy [FASSE Proxy Settings – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings/). **What to do:** 1. **Ask the data provider if they can allowlist the proxy IP** rather than a single compute node IP. You can find the proxy’s public IP by running `curl ifconfig.me` or similar from a FASSE node. 2. **Check if the provider allows allowlisting by domain or range** instead of a single IP. 3. If the provider insists on a specific IP and won’t accept the proxy IP, you may need to: - Download the data on your local machine and transfer it to FASSE, or - Use a different access method (e.g., a VPN connection from your own machine) that uses an IP you can control. **Note:** The proxy does not allow *all* traffic, so ensure the data provider’s domain/port is accessible through `rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu:3128` [FASSE Proxy Settings – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings/).
✅ Expected Answer
140.247.232.228. All traffic to the Internet from FASSE — VPN, login, compute, or fasseood — goes out through the FASRC proxy (rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu), which currently has that external IP address. It is subject to change, so you can confirm it by running `curl icanhazip.com` from the node you will actually be working on.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Question 54: question_54

❓ Question

Our data use agreement says the data has to be encrypted at rest. I was planning to stage the intermediate files for my FASSE jobs in netscratch since it's fast. Is that OK?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sharing-for-publications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I cannot confirm whether **netscratch** provides encryption at rest for sensitive data governed by a Data Use Agreement (DUA). Here's what the documentation does cover: - **Data Use Agreements (DUA):** Before any data considered confidential, proprietary, or sensitive can be stored on the FASRC cluster, it must be properly classified and any data use agreements must be in place and available [Data and Data Use Agreements (DUA)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements/). The project PI is responsible for ensuring compliance with any DUA. - **FASSE Protected Data:** For FASSE specifically, you are responsible for complying with applicable Harvard Information Security Policy (controls that apply to DSL3 and lower), Harvard Research Data Security Policy, and any applicable contracts/data use agreements [FASSE / Protected Data Transfers](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers/). - **Data Storage Offerings:** FASRC is transitioning to new storage infrastructure, but the documentation doesn't specify encryption-at-rest details for netscratch specifically [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). **Recommendation:** Since your DUA requires encryption at rest, you should: 1. **Contact FASRC Data Security** to confirm whether netscratch provides encryption at rest for sensitive/DUA-governed data 2. **Verify with your PI** that they've confirmed the storage location meets DUA requirements 3. **Consider alternative storage** if netscratch doesn't meet the encryption-at-rest requirement (e.g., encrypted volumes or dedicated secure storage) The documentation doesn't explicitly state whether netscratch encrypts data at rest, so this is a critical question to resolve before staging sensitive files there.
✅ Expected Answer
No. If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data the DUA applies to — neither local scratch nor FASRC's global scratch supports encryption at rest. Separately, DSL 3 / FASSE data should not be stored in your home directory either. You are responsible for knowing and complying with the applicable Harvard Information Security Policy, the Harvard Research Data Security Policy, and any applicable contracts / data use agreements; if you are unsure how this applies to your data, consult FASRC at security@rc.fas.harvard.edu or your School Security Officer.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Question 55: question_55

❓ Question

Our level 3 project storage doesn't show up as a collection in Globus at all. What do we have to do to get it added?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, **Data security level 3 storage shares are not automatically added to Globus** [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). To get your level 3 project storage added, you need to **contact FASRC** directly. The documentation states: > "If you find that your lab share is not seen by Globus or these sub directories don't exist in your lab's share, in the Holyoke or Boston collections, please contact us." When you contact them, specify that you need your **level 3 (secure) lab share** added to the appropriate Globus collection: - **"Harvard FAS RC Holyoke Secure"** for Holyoke data center - **"Harvard FAS RC Boston Secure"** for Boston data center Also note that Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` or `Users/$USER` — anything outside those directories (like `Everyone` or `Transfer`) is not available through Globus.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is broken: DSL 3 / FASSE storage is intentionally not included in Globus by default. To have it exposed, first review the governing DUA — Globus makes data available over the Internet without a VPN or private network, so a DUA requiring VPN/private-network access for all access to the data would not be Globus-compliant (consult your School Security Officer if unsure). Then submit a ticket to rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu that (1) includes the path to the project to add (e.g. /n/piname_project_l3), (2) states that the PI attests Globus is compliant with any contracts/DUAs governing the data, and (3) is either sent by the PI or receives a direct reply from the PI confirming it.

Question 56: question_56

❓ Question

How much CPU and RAM am I actually allowed on the FASRC login server, and is there a cap on how many terminal windows I can have open at once?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-from-abroad, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here are the resource limits on the login nodes: ### CPU and RAM Limits The Rocky 8 login nodes use **cgroups** to restrict each logged-in account to: - **1 CPU core** - **4 GB of RAM** This is a system-level enforcement designed to prevent any single user from hogging resources on these shared multi-user gateways. If you need more CPU or memory, you should submit an interactive job via `salloc` instead of running processes directly on the login node [Rocky 8 Transition Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide/). ### Terminal Windows The documentation does not specify a hard cap on the number of terminal/SSH sessions you can have open at once. However, the login nodes are intended only for light tasks like launching jobs, monitoring jobs, and preparing your environment—not for running heavy computations or keeping many persistent sessions open. For graphical applications, FASRC recommends using [Open OnDemand](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/) instead. If you run into session limits or need to check your current resource usage, you can use commands like `ulimit` or `sinfo`/`squeue` to monitor allocations.
✅ Expected Answer
Each login-node session is limited to 1 core and 8GB of memory, and you may have at most 5 sessions per user. Users who abuse the login nodes may have their sessions terminated, and the login nodes are rebooted during monthly maintenance to clear out stale sessions. If you need more than 5 sessions, switch to batch jobs, use Open OnDemand (which gives you a desktop with multiple terminals on a dedicated compute node), or use screen/tmux to multiplex one session.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Based on the FASRC documentation, here are the resource limits on the login nodes: ### CPU and RAM Limits The Rocky 8 login nodes use **cgroups** to restrict each logged-in account to: - **1 CPU core** - **4 GB of RAM** This is a system-level enforcement designed to prevent any single user from hogging resources on these shared multi-user gateways. If you need more CPU or memory, you should submit an interactive job via `salloc` instead of running processes directly on the login node [Rocky 8 Transition Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide/). ### Terminal Windows The documentation does not specify a hard cap on the number of terminal/SSH sessions you can have open at once. However, the login nodes are intended only for light tasks like launching jobs, monitoring jobs, and preparing your environment—not for running heavy computations or keeping many persistent sessions open. For graphical applications, FASRC recommends using [Open OnDemand](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/) instead. If you run into session limits or need to check your current resource usage, you can use commands like `ulimit` or `sinfo`/`squeue` to monitor allocations.

Question 57: question_57

❓ Question

I ran ssh-copy-id and my key works great when I'm in the office, but from my apartment it still makes me punch in the 6-digit code every single time. Did the key not install properly?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-key-error, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/using-ssh-controlmaster-for-single-sign-on, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/synchronizing-clocks

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
It sounds like your SSH key **did** install correctly — the fact that it works without a 6-digit code from the office suggests the key authentication is functioning as intended. The 6-digit code you're being prompted for is **not** related to the SSH key itself, but rather to **multi-factor authentication (MFA)** for Harvard Key (your NetID). Here's what's likely happening: - **On the Harvard network (office):** Your connection is recognized as coming from inside the Harvard network, so MFA may be skipped or handled differently. - **Off the Harvard network (apartment):** MFA is required for every login, which is why you're prompted for the 6-digit code each time. This is expected behavior — SSH keys authenticate *who you are*, but MFA adds an additional layer of verification for your Harvard Key credentials. The two are separate mechanisms. ### What you can do 1. **Check if you have an SSH agent running** that caches your key passphrase (if your key is passphrase-protected). This would skip the passphrase prompt but **not** the MFA code. 2. **Verify your SSH key is actually being used** by checking the server-side logs or running `ssh -v` to confirm key-based auth is succeeding before MFA kicks in. 3. **Look into Harvard's MFA settings** — some setups allow trusted devices or networks to reduce MFA prompts. Check the [Harvard Key MFA documentation](https://it.harvard.com/services/accounts/harvard-key/mfa/) for options to configure trusted devices or networks. If you'd like, I can help you troubleshoot further — for example, checking whether your SSH key is actually being offered during the connection.
✅ Expected Answer
The key is fine — this is by design. Passwordless (ssh-key) authentication is only permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks, including Harvard/FASRC VPNs. If you connect from an external network you are still required to provide two-factor authentication regardless of your key. So either connect to the VPN from home, or accept the 2FA prompt. (On FASSE, passwordless ssh is permitted from the FASSE VPN only.) You can confirm the key installed by checking that it is present in ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the cluster.

Question 59: question_59

❓ Question

My pandas script keeps blowing up with a memory error because the file is bigger than the node's RAM. Does FASRC want me to rewrite it, or can I keep using pandas somehow?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/Example2/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/pbdMPI/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/R/Example2/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/pbdMPI/run_scatter.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You don’t need to rewrite your script from scratch—pandas can handle files larger than node RAM if you process them in **chunks**. This is the standard approach FASRC recommends for memory-bound pandas workflows. ### Quick fix: use `chunksize` in `pd.read_csv()` ```python chunks = pd.read_csv('large_file.csv', chunksize=100_000) results = [] for chunk in chunks: # process each chunk (filter, aggregate, transform, etc.) results.append(process(chunk)) final = pd.concat(results) ``` Key tips: - **`chunksize`** controls how many rows are loaded into memory at once. Start with a value that fits comfortably within your job’s memory limit (e.g., 50k–500k rows, depending on columns). - **Aggregate incrementally** instead of concatenating all chunks at the end. For example, sum or count per chunk and combine the partial results, which avoids holding everything in RAM. - Make sure your SLURM job requests enough memory with `--mem=`. The default is only 100 MB, so you’ll likely need something like `--mem=16G` or more, depending on your workload [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). ### If you still hit memory issues: - **Reduce columns** with `usecols` in `read_csv()`. - **Use `dtype`** to specify smaller data types (e.g., `float32` instead of `float64`). - Consider **Dask** or **Polars** for out-of-core or more memory-efficient alternatives, though pandas with chunks is usually sufficient for moderate overflows. Want help estimating a good `chunksize` for your file, or adapting your specific script?
✅ Expected Answer
Both options exist, and which one FASRC recommends depends on scale. Start with chunking. For moderately large datasets, Dask can enhance Pandas by parallelizing operations — you can convert a Pandas DataFrame to a Dask DataFrame with dd.from_pandas() and keep most of your existing code. But for very large datasets that exceed memory constraints, using Dask alone as a substitute for Pandas is the more effective solution: Dask is a drop-in replacement with a familiar DataFrame API that works lazily, breaking work into smaller tasks that only execute when you call .compute(). Separately, switching from CSV to a columnar format like Parquet (e.g. df.to_parquet('output_data.parquet', compression='snappy')) greatly improves I/O performance.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 60: question_60

❓ Question

I switched to the newest Intel module on the cluster and now the compiler command I've always used doesn't exist anymore. What am I supposed to call to build my C file now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fortran, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise2/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/WRF_Model/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you've loaded an Intel module version **24.0.0 or newer**, the old `icc` command has been replaced by **`icx`** (the Intel oneAPI DPC++/C++ Compiler). To compile a C file, use: ```bash module load intel/24.0.0-fasrc01 # or whichever 24.x version you loaded icx -O2 -o myprogram myprogram.c ``` The mapping is: | Intel Compiler Version | C Compiler | Fortran Compiler | C++ Compiler | |------------------------|------------|------------------|--------------| | Below 24.0.0 | `icc` | `ifort` | `icpc` | | 24.0.0 and above | **`icx`** | **`ifx`** | **`icpx`** | See the full details at [C Programming Language – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). If you'd rather stick with the older `icc` command, you can load an older Intel module (e.g., `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`) instead.
✅ Expected Answer
Intel renamed the compiler binaries at version 24.0.0. For Intel modules below 24.0.0 the commands are icc (C), ifortran (Fortran), and icpc (C++). For Intel 24.0.0 and above they are icx (C), ifx (Fortran), and icpx (C++). So with a newer Intel module you must invoke icx instead of icc. (You can list the available Intel compilers with `module spider intel` and load a specific one, e.g. `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`.)

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 61: question_61

❓ Question

Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No to both.** Here's why: 1. **Don't compile on login nodes.** FASRC recommends compiling on a compute node instead, since compilation can take from a few seconds up to a minute depending on code complexity. Use an interactive job on a compute node (e.g., via `salloc`) or the `test` partition to compile and test your program before running production jobs. 2. **Don't put the `gcc` compile command in your `sbatch` script.** FASRC explicitly states it is best practice to compile separately and then use the resulting executable in your production batch job. Including the compile command in the `sbatch` script will **recompile your program every time you submit the job**, which wastes time and resources. **Recommended workflow:** 1. Compile your code separately on a compute node (using `module load gcc` or `module load intel` as needed). 2. Verify the executable works. 3. Submit your production run as a batch job (`sbatch`) that simply runs the pre-compiled executable. 4. If you change the source code, recompile separately again, then resubmit the batch job. See the [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/) page for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
No on both counts. FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile a C program (compilation can take a few seconds to a minute depending on the code's complexity), and specifically recommends using the `test` partition to compile and test the program before running the production job. Best practice is to compile the code separately and then use the resulting executable in your sbatch script — avoid putting the compilation command inside the sbatch script, because that recompiles the program on every submission. If you change the source, recompile separately and then resubmit the production batch job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
**No to both.** Here's why: 1. **Don't compile on login nodes.** FASRC recommends compiling on a compute node instead, since compilation can take from a few seconds up to a minute depending on code complexity. Use an interactive job on a compute node (e.g., via `salloc`) or the `test` partition to compile and test your program before running production jobs. 2. **Don't put the `gcc` compile command in your `sbatch` script.** FASRC explicitly states it is best practice to compile separately and then use the resulting executable in your production batch job. Question 62: question_62

❓ Question

I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/Notebook.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gnu-screen, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
There is **no single built-in command** to completely uninstall Julia on the FASRC cluster. The official installation method uses `juliaup`, which installs Julia into your home directory and modifies your shell startup files. To fully remove it, you need to clean up a few things manually: ### 1. Remove the Julia installation directory The installer places Julia in a versioned directory under `$HOME` (e.g., `~/.juliaup/` or `~/julia-*`). Remove it with: ```bash rm -rf ~/.juliaup ``` ### 2. Remove Julia packages and cache Julia stores packages and caches in your home directory under: ```bash rm -rf ~/.julia ``` ### 3. Clean up shell configuration The installer adds a `juliaup` initialization line to your `~/.bashrc` (or `~/.zshrc`). Open the file and remove the line that sources `juliaup`, typically something like: ```bash # Remove or comment out this line: eval "$($HOME/.juliaup/bin/juliaup shell-init bash)" ``` ### 4. Verify removal After cleanup, run: ```bash which julia ``` It should return nothing. If it still finds Julia, check your `$PATH` or any other shell config files (`~/.profile`, `~/.bash_profile`, etc.) for leftover references. ### Does it leave junk behind? Yes, if you only run `juliaup self uninstall` (if available) or remove the binary, it can leave behind: - `~/.julia` (packages, caches, registries) - Modified shell config files (`~/.bashrc`, `~/.zshrc`) - Potentially `~/.local/share/julia` or other hidden directories So a thorough cleanup requires removing both the installation directory and the `~/.julia` package/cache directory, plus editing your shell startup files. For reference, see the [Julia Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia/) documentation on the FASRC website.
✅ Expected Answer
Run `juliaup self uninstall`. That is the uninstall command, but it does not always clean everything up: afterward, check for the `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup` directories and delete them manually if they are still there. (This matters because the default Julia install location is $HOME, and the installer also adds Juliaup initialization to your ~/.bashrc to put `julia` on your $PATH.)

Question 63: question_63

❓ Question

I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unix-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue is that **directory permissions also matter**. Even if you `chmod 777` a file, your collaborator still needs **read and execute (`r-x`) permissions on every parent directory** leading to that file to traverse into it. Here's what's likely happening: 1. **The top-level lab directory/share** is probably set to something like `drwxr-x---` or `drwxr-s---`, which means **`Other` (everyone not in your lab group) has no access** to the directory itself. 2. Even if the file inside is `777` (world-readable), the collaborator can't get *into* the directory to reach it. As the FASRC documentation explains: *"Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share. Otherwise, it has no access at all"* [Understanding file and group permissions](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions/). ### What to do instead Rather than opening up permissions broadly (which is discouraged, especially on FASSE/L3 shares), consider these options: - **Use Globus** to share files with external collaborators without giving them system accounts [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). - **Add the collaborator to a shared group** if they have an FASRC account. - **Use SCP/SFTP** if they have an account on the system. Would you like help setting up one of these alternatives?
✅ Expected Answer
Making the file world-readable is not enough on its own. For a user in the 'Other' category to reach a file, they must first be able to traverse into the top-level share/directory that contains it. If the top level of the lab directory or share is set drwxr-x---, drwxrwx---, or drwxr-s--- then Other has no access to the share at all, even if a file inside it is set -rwxrwxrwx. Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share; otherwise it has no access. (FASSE/L3 shares should never allow Other access.) To share with someone outside the group, the enclosing directory must permit Other traversal - e.g. the 'Everyone' directory that lab shares provide for exactly this purpose - or the person can be added to your lab Group by your PI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 64: question_64

❓ Question

My labmate keeps his personal scratch folder under Users/ in our old lab share, but our newly created share and netscratch have no Users folder. Where am I supposed to put my own personal directory now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On FASRC, **there is no longer a `Users/` folder** inside your lab share or netscratch. The old `Users/` structure was part of the legacy storage layout. In the new storage model: - **Lab Storage** (`/n/<lab_name>/Lab/`) is your primary shared working directory. Individual users typically create their own personal subdirectories directly under `/n/<lab_name>/Lab/` (e.g., `/n/<lab_name>/Lab/your_username/`), rather than under a `Users/` subfolder. - **Netscratch** (`/n/netscratch/<lab_name>/`) is temporary, high-performance scratch space intended for transient job data. It is **not backed up** and files older than 90 days may be purged. Personal directories are not typically placed here unless you're storing active job output. **What to do now:** 1. Create your personal directory directly under your lab's Lab storage: ```bash mkdir -p /n/<your_lab_name>/Lab/your_username ``` 2. Set appropriate permissions so only your lab group can access it: ```bash chgrp <your_lab_group> /n/<your_lab_name>/Lab/your_username chmod 770 /n/<your_lab_name>/Lab/your_username ``` For more details on the new storage structure, see the [Storage Modernization Initiative FAQs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs/) and [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/).
✅ Expected Answer
The Users directory only exists on shares created prior to October 2024. On new shares and on /n/netscratch there is no Users directory - FASRC says to use the Lab directory to create personal folders instead. The Lab directory is set drwxrws--- so that every member of the lab group has read/write/execute access to share data amongst the group, and it is the preferred place both for personal folders on new shares and for sharing data with your lab group. On the older shares, the sub-directories in the Users directory are owned by individuals and by default only allow execute access to the group.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

I log into the FASRC web portal in my browser, it accepts my credentials, and then it just spins forever and never shows the menus. Restarting the browser doesn't help. What can I do myself before emailing support?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
I don't see specific troubleshooting steps for a spinning FASRC portal login in the indexed documentation. However, here are some general browser-side steps you can try before emailing support: 1. **Clear your browser cache and cookies** for the portal site — stale or corrupted cached files are a common cause of pages that hang after login. In most browsers you can do this via Settings → Privacy/History → Clear browsing data. 2. **Try a private/incognito window** — this bypasses extensions and cached data. If the portal loads there, the issue is likely an extension or cached content. 3. **Disable browser extensions** temporarily — some ad-blockers or privacy extensions can interfere with portal scripts. 4. **Try a different browser** (or update your current one) — this helps isolate whether the problem is browser-specific. 5. **Check your network connection** — a slow or intermittent connection can cause the portal to hang during the authentication handshake. If none of these resolve the issue, you can email FASRC support at **help@fasrc.harvard.edu** with details about your browser, OS, and any error messages you see in the browser's developer console (press F12 to open it).
✅ Expected Answer
If the browser hangs for a long time after authenticating without displaying the Open OnDemand dashboard, restart your Per-User NGINX (PUN) process by visiting: - Cannon: https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ - FASSE: https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ Also note that the very first login can legitimately take some time while OOD sets up files and cache in your home directory; that Avast security software can cause 'no internet', 'read error', and 'the requested URL could not be retrieved' messages (disable Web Shield); and that Chrome or Firefox are recommended over Safari and Internet Explorer.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

Question 67: question_67

❓ Question

My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parallel-matlab-pct-dcs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/MATLAB/Example1/run.sbatch, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/OpenMP/Example2/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you request `-c 8` (or `--cpus-per-task=8`) in your SLURM batch script, SLURM sets the environment variable **`SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`** to `8` inside your job. You should read this variable inside your R script to determine how many parallel workers to spawn, rather than hard-coding a number. ### How to do it in R You can read the variable directly from the environment: ```r n_workers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) ``` Then pass `n_workers` to your parallel backend (e.g., `parallel::makeCluster(n_workers)`, `future::plan(multisession, workers = n_workers)`, or `foreach` with `.workers = n_workers`). ### Why this matters If your script ignores `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` and instead spawns, say, 32 workers on an 8-core allocation, you oversubscribe the node. This causes severe contention, thrashing, and poor performance — exactly the "grinding to a halt" you're seeing. By tying your worker count to the SLURM environment variable, your job respects the resources it actually requested. ### Quick checklist 1. **In your `.sbatch` script**, request the right number of cores: ```bash #SBATCH -c 8 ``` 2. **In your R script**, read the variable: ```r n_workers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) ``` 3. **Pass `n_workers`** to your parallelization function. 4. **Verify** with `echo $SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` in a test job or by printing it in R before launching workers. This pattern is also used in other languages on FASRC clusters — for example, Python and MATLAB examples on GitHub cap their worker/thread counts using `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` to avoid oversubscription [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py).
✅ Expected Answer
Do not use parallel::detectCores() to determine the number of CPUs/processes to use in your job - it detects the number of physical CPU cores on the compute node, not the number allocated to your job, which is why you end up oversubscribed. Use parallelly::availableCores() instead, which returns the number of CPUs allocated to your Slurm job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel
💬 Agent Messages (7)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: R parallel workers Slurm environment variable SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK detect cores
🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: R parallel detect number of cores SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK
🛠️ Tool Call #4: search_local_files
Args: SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK R parallel
🛠️ Tool Call #5: search_local_files
Args: SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK
🛠️ Tool Call #6: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: R parallel detect cores SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK environment variable
🤖 Assistant Message #7
When you request `-c 8` (or `--cpus-per-task=8`) in your SLURM batch script, SLURM sets the environment variable **`SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`** to `8` inside your job. You should read this variable inside your R script to determine how many parallel workers to spawn, rather than hard-coding a number. ### How to do it in R You can read the variable directly from the environment: ```r n_workers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) ``` Then pass `n_workers` to your parallel backend (e.g., `parallel::makeCluster(n_workers)`, `future::plan(multisession, workers = n_workers)`, or `foreach` with `.workers = n_workers`). ### Why this matters If your script ignores `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` and instead spawns, say, 32 workers on an 8-core allocation, you oversubscribe the node. This causes severe contention, thrashing, and poor performance — exactly the "grinding to a halt" you're seeing. By tying your worker count to the SLURM environment variable, your job respects the resources it actually requested. ### Quick checklist 1. **In your `.sbatch` script**, request the right number of cores: ```bash #SBATCH -c 8 ``` 2. **In your R script**, read the variable: ```r n_workers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) ``` 3. **Pass `n_workers`** to your parallelization function. 4. **Verify** with `echo $SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` in a test job or by printing it in R before launching workers. This pattern is also used in other languages on FASRC clusters — for example, Python and MATLAB examples on GitHub cap their worker/thread counts using `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK` to avoid oversubscription [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py).

Question 68: question_68

❓ Question

I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/Example2/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parigp, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You haven't corrupted your environment — this is expected behavior. On the FASRC cluster, **MPI implementations are mutually exclusive** in the module system. When you load `mpich` after `openmpi`, the module system replaces the MPI library in your environment rather than stacking both. This is by design: mixing MPI implementations can cause linking conflicts and runtime errors, since both provide the same MPI interface symbols. ### Can you keep both around? You can **load either one at a time**, but not both simultaneously in the same shell session. To switch back to OpenMPI, simply run: ```bash module unload mpich module load openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01 # or whichever version you need ``` If you need both in a single workflow (e.g., one tool built against OpenMPI and another against MPICH), the recommended approach is to use **separate conda environments** or **separate shell sessions**, each with its own MPI module loaded. For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/).
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is corrupted - this is intentional. The modules are set up so that you can only have one MPI module loaded at a time; if you try loading a second one it will automatically unload the first, which is done to avoid dependency collisions. So you cannot keep both loaded: pick one implementation (OpenMPI or MPICH, each built with either the GNU or the Intel compiler suite) and load it with its matching compiler module, e.g. module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01. Note also that there is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in - you must load one.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 69: question_69

❓ Question

I need mpi4py in a conda environment, but it has to be built against the same OpenMPI my Fortran code uses. I just ran mamba create with mpi4py in the package list - what MPI did that actually give me, and how do I get OpenMPI instead?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vdi-apps, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Performance/TAU/Example1/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/PyTorch/setup_environment.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rstudio-server-vs-rstudio-desktop, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-and-rstudio

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you ran `mamba create` with `mpi4py` in the package list, **conda installed MPICH** by default as the MPI library that `mpi4py` links against. If your Fortran code uses **OpenMPI**, you need to rebuild `mpi4py` against that same OpenMPI so the two libraries are compatible. Here's how to do it on the FASRC cluster: 1. **Load the compiler and OpenMPI modules** (pick versions that match your Fortran setup): ```bash module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01 module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03 ``` 2. **Load a Python module**: ```bash module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01 ``` 3. **Create a conda environment without `mpi4py`** (so conda doesn't pull in MPICH): ```bash mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel source activate python3_env2 ``` 4. **Install `mpi4py` via `pip`** (this will detect the loaded OpenMPI and build against it): ```bash pip install mpi4py ``` This approach ensures `mpi4py` is compiled against the same OpenMPI that your Fortran code uses, avoiding ABI mismatches. For reference, see the FASRC documentation on [MPI & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface).
✅ Expected Answer
Putting mpi4py in the mamba create package list (e.g. mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py) also installs an MPI library required by mpi4py, and by default that is MPICH - not OpenMPI. If your workflow requires a specific flavor and/or version of MPI, install mpi4py with pip into a custom conda environment instead: load the compiler and MPI modules first (module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01; module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03), load a Python module (module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01), create the environment WITHOUT mpi4py (mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel), activate it (source activate python3_env2), then run pip install mpi4py so it builds against the loaded OpenMPI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 70: question_70

❓ Question

I used the 'copy mount path to clipboard' button in Starfish and got /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab, but when I cd to it on a login node I get 'No such file or directory'. What's the correct path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The path `/n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` is a **Starfish-specific mount path** used for the Starfish web interface and desktop/laptop mounting (via Samba). It is **not** the path you should use on FASRC login or compute nodes. On FASRC clusters (login and compute nodes), the correct path to your lab's holylabs storage is: ``` /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab ``` This is the standard path documented for accessing holylabs storage on FASRC systems [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). **Key distinction:** - **On FASRC nodes (login/compute):** Use `/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab` - **On your local desktop/laptop (via Samba mount):** Use `/n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` Most FASRC file systems, including holylabs, **cannot** be mounted on your desktop, so the `vast-holylabs` path is only available through the Starfish interface or specific Samba mounts [Mounting Storage on Desktop or Laptop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
The path Starfish copies is not directly usable - you must make two edits: remove 'vast-' and remove '/C'. So /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab becomes /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab. The letter C indicates the filesystem is on the Cannon cluster; a group share on FASSE would show F instead of C before the group name.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 71: question_71

❓ Question

I want to drag a few files out of my home directory into my lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface, but my home directory doesn't show up as an endpoint in Globus. What else can I use?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Good news: you **can** move files from your home directory to your lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface — you just need to use a different tool than Globus. Here are your options: ### 1. Globus Connect Personal (for drag-and-drop from your local machine) If you want to drag files from your **local computer** into your lab's netscratch folder via Globus, you can install **Globus Connect Personal** on your laptop/desktop. This creates a "Mapped Collection" on your local machine that appears in Globus alongside the FASRC endpoints. You can then drag files from your local machine directly into your lab share via the Globus web interface or desktop app. See [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) for setup instructions. ### 2. Globus for lab-to-lab transfers (not home directory) Globus **does not** support home directories — they are explicitly excluded for security reasons (authentication keys/passwords could be inadvertently shared). However, your lab's netscratch folder (`/n/netscratch/...`) **is** available via Globus under the "Harvard FAS RC" endpoint. So if you first copy files from your home directory to your lab share (using a command-line tool like `cp` or `rsync`), you can then use Globus to move them from there to other locations. ### 3. Command-line alternatives for home → netscratch Since both your home directory and netscratch are on the FASRC cluster, the simplest way to move files is via the command line: - `cp` or `rsync` from your home directory to your lab's netscratch folder. ### Summary - **Home directories are not available in Globus** — this is intentional for security. - **Lab shares (including netscratch) are available in Globus.** - For point-and-click transfers involving your home directory, use **Globus Connect Personal** on your local machine to drag files into your lab share, or use command-line tools (`cp`/`rsync`) to move files within the cluster. See [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
Globus does not have access to home directories, which is why yours does not appear. For transfers to or from your home directory, use an Open OnDemand Remote Desktop session instead: go to the OOD dashboard (Cannon: rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, FASSE: fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu), start a Remote Desktop session, open a File Manager by clicking the 'Home' folder on the desktop (or Applications -> File Manager), open a second File Manager window, navigate one to the source (e.g. /n/home01/jharvard/) and the other to the destination (e.g. /n/netscratch/jharvard_lab/Lab/), then right-click the source file -> Copy and right-click the destination window -> Paste (drag-and-drop works too, but behaves like cut-and-paste). Command-line tools (cp, mv, rsync, fpsync) are the alternative; Globus is only recommended for transfers between different filesystems, not within one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, your collaborator can get an account even without a HarvardKey. Here's what you need to know: ### How she can qualify - If she is a **faculty member at another university** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should choose **EXTERNAL** during account sign-up. - If she is a **student or researcher at another university or company** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should also choose **EXTERNAL** [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). ### What you (the PI) need to keep doing - **Authorize her access**: Her account must be authorized by you (the Harvard PI) or the management of the FAS. You are responsible for approving external collaborators. - **She must agree to Harvard policies**: All account holders, including outside collaborators, agree to be held accountable by Harvard's Electronic Access and Information Security policies [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). - **She must subscribe to notifications**: All active FAS RC account holders are subscribed to the notifications mailing list, which is a requirement for all users [Acceptable Use Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/). ### Important notes - Cluster access is intended only for legitimate purposes that benefit research at Harvard University [Acceptable Use Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/). - She will need to go through the account request tool and select **EXTERNAL** as her affiliation type [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). If you have any questions about the authorization process, you may want to contact FASRC directly for guidance on managing external collaborator accounts.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Question 73: question_73

❓ Question

I'm a new PI with a HarvardKey but the system won't let me approve my grad student's FASRC account request. What do I need to do?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/get-a-fasse-account-and-project-group

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common issue for new PIs. Here’s what’s likely happening and how to fix it: ### 1. You may not be recognized as a PI in the system yet The account request tool relies on your Harvard appointment being linked to your HarvardKey. If you’re a new PI, your appointment may not yet be reflected in the system that the account request tool uses to identify eligible sponsors. ### 2. Your grad student may have selected the wrong job type As noted in the account request documentation, if the student selected **FACULTY** as their job type but you don’t have an active Harvard faculty appointment (e.g., you’re a postdoc or researcher with PI-like rights), the system may not recognize you as an eligible approver. The student should select **STAFF** or **POSTDOC** instead, unless you hold an actual Harvard faculty appointment. ### 3. What to do next - **Check your appointment status**: Confirm that your PI appointment is active and visible in Harvard’s systems. - **Have the student resubmit** with the correct job type if needed. - **Contact FASRC directly** if the issue persists — they can manually verify your PI status and approve the request on their end. You can reach them through the [FASRC Help Portal](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/) or by submitting a ticket. ### Key resource - [Request an FAS Research Computing Account](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/)
✅ Expected Answer
Complete the FAS Onboard tool for approvers at https://onboard.rc.fas.harvard.edu/onboard/. The onboarding checklist states that if you have a HarvardKey but are denied access to approve new accounts, you should visit and complete the FAS Onboard tool for approvers.
