Question 74: question_74

❓ Question

We're wrapping up a project and want to push the raw data off to tape. Before we start, what constraints are there on how the data has to be laid out, and what does it cost per year?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know about moving data to cold storage (tape): ## Data layout constraints - **Tar archives required**: You must create tar archives of your data before submitting to cold storage. Individual files are not accepted directly. - **Maximum archive size**: Each tar archive can be up to **2 TB** in size. - **No compression**: Archives should **not** be compressed (e.g., no `.tar.gz`). Use uncompressed tar files only. - **File naming**: Archives should have a `.tar` extension. ## Cost - **$1,000 per TB per year** for cold storage allocation. - You request storage through a **Storage Allocation Request** in the FASRC portal, specifying the amount of TB you need. ## Process overview 1. **Create tar archives** of your data (up to 2 TB each, uncompressed). 2. **Submit a Storage Allocation Request** via the FASRC portal for the amount of cold storage you need. 3. Once approved, you'll receive instructions for transferring your tar archives to the cold storage system. ## Important notes - Cold storage is intended for **long-term archival** of data you don't need frequent access to. Retrieval from tape can take time (typically several business days), so plan accordingly if you anticipate needing to restore data. - Make sure your data is complete and verified before archiving, since retrieval is not instantaneous. For the most current details on the submission process and any updates to policies, I'd recommend checking the [FASRC Cold Storage documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cold-storage/) or contacting FASRC support directly.
✅ Expected Answer
Tape (NESE) storage is sold in 20TB increments, allows ten thousand files per folder, and requires file sizes between 1GiB and 100GiB. It costs $15/yr per TB, is externally managed by the Northeast Storage Exchange (NESE), and data is transferred to tape using Globus. It is intended for long-term storage of inactive research data after project completion or for data retention purposes.

❓ Question

I put a dataset in the Everyone folder so another lab could grab it, but it never shows up in Globus. Is Globus broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Globus isn't broken—this is expected behavior. The **Everyone** folder is **not available on Globus** and is intended only for internal sharing among users on the HPC cluster [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). If you want another lab to access your data via Globus, you'll need to place the dataset in your **Lab** folder instead, which is the directory shared out via Globus by default [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). Would you like help moving your data to your Lab folder or setting up Globus access for your collaborators?
✅ Expected Answer
No, that is expected behavior. The Everyone subdirectory is visible to anyone on the HPC cluster and is intended only for internal sharing with other labs on the cluster — it is not available on Globus. The Lab subdirectory is the one shared out via Globus, so data that has to move over Globus needs to live under Lab.

❓ Question

Is there already a samtools container sitting on the cluster somewhere, or do I have to pull one from Docker Hub myself?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG15/shifter.pdf, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/macaulay2, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, there is no mention of a pre-installed `samtools` container on the FASRC cluster. The documentation indicates that users typically need to pull or build their own containers. Here are your options: 1. **Pull from Docker Hub:** You can pull an existing Singularity image from the SingularityCE Container Library or build a SIF file from an OCI container image located in Docker Hub or another OCI container registry (e.g., quay.io, NVIDIA NGC Catalog) [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster/). 2. **Check for a Software Module:** While not explicitly mentioned in the container docs, it's worth checking if `samtools` is available as a standard software module on the cluster, which would be the easiest option if available. You can check this by running `module avail samtools` or searching the software catalog. 3. **Build Your Own:** If you need a specific version or configuration, you can build a SIF file from a Singularity definition file or an OCI Dockerfile [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster/). If you'd like, I can help you search for `samtools` as a software module or provide instructions on how to pull/build a container for it.
✅ Expected Answer
There is one already on the cluster. Cluster nodes automount a CernVM-File System at /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/, which provides Singularity images for the BioContainers project (auto-generated from Bioconda packages). Images follow the layout /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/FIRST_LETTER/SECOND_LETTER/PACKAGE_NAME:VERSION--CONDA_BUILD, for example: `singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help`. Expect a 10-30 second delay the first time the path is accessed on a compute node that has not mounted/cached it.

Question 77: question_77

❓ Question

A spack install blew up and by the time I looked the build log was already gone. How do I stop spack from throwing the logs away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/augustus-install.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/perl, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Spack stores build logs in a temporary directory under your Spack staging area (typically something like `~/.spack/staging/.cache/build_logs/` or within the build prefix of the package). By default, Spack **does not delete** these logs after a build — they persist in the build prefix and in the staging cache. If your logs are disappearing, here are the most likely causes and fixes: ### 1. Check the build prefix directly Each package has a build directory under: ``` ~/.spack/staging/<package_name>/<version>/<hash>/ ``` Look for files like `install.out`, `install.log`, or `build.log` inside that directory. ### 2. Check the global build log cache Spack also caches build logs in: ``` ~/.spack/staging/.cache/build_logs/ ``` These logs are keyed by build hash and should persist across rebuilds. ### 3. If using `spack install --keep-stage` If you're using `--keep-stage`, Spack will retain the entire build stage directory (including logs) instead of cleaning it up. This is useful for debugging: ```bash spack install --keep-stage <package> ``` ### 4. Check if a cleanup job is running Spack does **not** automatically delete logs after a build. If logs are being removed, something else may be cleaning up: - A cron job or script that purges `~/.spack/staging/` - A container or environment that resets on logout - Manual cleanup (e.g., `spack clean`) You can check for active cleanup with: ```bash spack clean --all # This removes build artifacts; avoid if you need logs ``` ### 5. Copy logs before they might be lost If you're worried about logs being lost, copy them immediately after a failed build: ```bash cp ~/.spack/staging/.cache/build_logs/* /path/to/keep/logs/ ``` If you can share more about your Spack setup (e.g., are you using a container, a shared cluster environment, or a custom Spack config?), I can help narrow down the cause.
✅ Expected Answer
Spack builds in a `stage` directory located in /tmp and cleans that space up once it finishes building, whether the build succeeded or failed — which is why the logs disappear. To keep them, first set the `$TMP` environment variable to a location you want the stage files dumped into, then add the `--keep-stage` flag, e.g. `spack install --keep-stage <package>`, which tells spack to keep the staging files rather than remove them.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Question 78: question_78

❓ Question

Software I built with spack runs fine on the node I built it on but won't run on nodes in other partitions. How do I build something that works everywhere on the cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue you're experiencing is likely due to **architecture-specific optimizations** or **missing dependencies** when building software with Spack. Here's how to ensure your builds work across all cluster partitions: ## Key Strategies for Portable Builds ### 1. **Avoid Architecture-Specific Optimizations** When building with Spack, avoid flags that tie your binaries to specific CPU features. Use generic architecture targets or let Spack handle this automatically. ### 2. **Use Spack Environments** Create and use Spack environments to manage dependencies consistently: ```bash spack env create myenv spack env activate myenv # Add your packages spack add your-package spack install ``` ### 3. **Check Your Build Environment** Ensure you're building on a node that represents the **lowest common denominator** across all partitions. Different partitions may have different CPU architectures or library versions. ### 4. **Verify Dependencies** Make sure all runtime dependencies are properly included in your Spack environment. Missing libraries can cause binaries to work on the build node but fail elsewhere. ## Recommended Approach 1. **Build on a standard compute node** (not a login node or special partition) 2. **Use Spack's built-in compiler abstraction** rather than custom compiler flags 3. **Test on multiple node types** before considering your build portable 4. **Document your Spack environment** for reproducibility For detailed Spack usage on FASRC clusters, see the [SPACK Package Manager documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager). Would you like help with a specific package or build configuration?
✅ Expected Answer
By default Spack autodetects the architecture of the hardware it is building on and optimizes the build for it, so a build made on one CPU generation may not run on another (the docs give the example that LAMMPS built on Sapphire Rapids may not run on Cascade Lake). If you plan to run across heterogeneous hardware/partitions, set Spack to a generic architecture: edit `etc/spack/packages.yaml` inside the spack folder (create it if it does not exist) and add: packages: all: target: [x86_64]
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: spack build portable binary cross partition compatible architecture
🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: spack build portable binary compatible all nodes cluster partitions

Question 79: question_79

❓ Question

I don't want spack spending hours compiling its own OpenMPI — how do I make it use the openmpi module we already have on the cluster, and is there anything I have to do before installing?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
# Using the cluster's OpenMPI module with Spack Spack can be told to use an **externally-provided** (system) OpenMPI instead of compiling its own. You do this by declaring it as an `externals` entry in your Spack configuration file. ## Step 1 — Load the OpenMPI module First, load the version of OpenMPI you want Spack to use: ```bash module load openmpi/<version> ``` Then verify the prefix (install path) so you can reference it: ```bash which mpirun # or module show openmpi/<version> ``` ## Step 2 — Add an externals entry for `openmpi` in `~/.spack/packages.yaml` Edit (or create) `~/.spack/packages.yaml` and add an `externals` block for the `openmpi` package. For example: ```yaml packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@<version> prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/Core/openmpi/<version>-fasrc01 ``` Replace `<version>` with the actual version (e.g., `4.1.6`) and the `prefix` with the path you found in Step 1. ## Step 3 — Tell Spack to use it When you install a package that depends on MPI, explicitly request the external: ```bash spack install <your-package> ^openmpi ``` Or, if you want Spack to **always** prefer the external, you can add: ```yaml packages: openmpi: buildable: false ``` This tells Spack it cannot build its own copy and must use the external. ## Before installing — a few checks 1. **Make sure the compiler Spack uses is compatible** with the OpenMPI module. If you're using a compiler from a module, you may need to declare it as an external compiler in `packages.yaml` as well (see the Spack docs on [adding compilers](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager/)). 2. **Verify the OpenMPI module provides the libraries** Spack needs (e.g., `libmpi.so`). You can check with `ldd $(which mpirun)` or `module show openmpi/<version>`. 3. **Run `spack spec <your-package> ^openmpi`** first to preview the dependency resolution before actually installing. --- If you tell me which version of OpenMPI is loaded on the cluster, I can help you craft the exact `packages.yaml` entry.
✅ Expected Answer
Configure the cluster's OpenMPI as an external package in `~/.spack/packages.yaml` (create the file if it does not exist). Get the prefix by loading the modules and reading $MPI_HOME, then add: packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@5.0.5%gcc@14.2.0 prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/Comp/gcc/14.2.0-fasrc01/openmpi/5.0.5-fasrc01 buildable: false The `buildable: false` option ensures MPI won't be built from source and Spack will use the module in that prefix instead. Before installing, run `module purge` — this is required or the build fails (e.g. `module purge` then `spack install hdf5@1.14.6 % gcc@14.2.0 ^ openmpi@5.0.5`).

Question 80: question_80

❓ Question

We cleaned out several TB of old data last month but this month's storage charge came in exactly the same. Is the bill wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spinal-expense-codes, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The bill is likely correct. Per FASRC's storage billing policy, **the monthly charge is based on the total size of your storage allocation (in TiB) and the storage tier, not on how much space you actually use** [Data Storage Billing](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing/). This means that simply deleting files will not reduce your monthly cost. To lower the charge, you would need to **reduce the allocation size itself** before the 15th of the month (the billing cutoff date). Changes made after the 15th will be reflected on the following month's bill. If you believe there's an error, you can contact the Storage Service Center at [billing@rc.fas.harvard.edu](mailto:billing@rc.fas.harvard.edu) within 3 business days of receiving the bill to review or dispute the charges.
✅ Expected Answer
No, the bill is correct. The monthly charge is based on the size of the storage allocation, not on the amount of storage actually used, so deleting files will not reduce the charge unless the allocation size itself is also reduced. Separately, the cutoff for billing changes is the 15th of each month — any change made to an allocation after the 15th is only reflected on the following month's bill.

Question 81: question_81

❓ Question

After the storage billing email lands, how long do we actually get to dispute the charges before they just go through?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You have **3 business days** from when the billing email is sent to review the charges, make adjustments, or contact the storage service center to dispute them. After 3 days, a lack of communication is considered approval and the bills will be processed. For billing questions or disputes, email [RC Storage Billing](mailto:billing@rc.fas.harvard.edu). You can also use the link in the billing email to approve records or make adjustments directly.
✅ Expected Answer
Three days. Once billing emails are sent, PIs or lab managers have 3 days to review the charges and approve them, make adjustments, or contact the storage service center to clear up any issues. After 3 days, a lack of communication is considered approval and the bills will be processed.

Question 82: question_82

❓ Question

Every time I try to map my Cannon home drive on my Windows laptop I end up locked out of my account. How many bad tries does that take, how long am I locked out for, and what am I doing wrong in the Map Network Drive box?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unlock_account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You're likely hitting a **password lockout** because Windows is repeatedly sending an incorrect username or password when it tries to auto-connect to the mapped drive at login. Here's what's going on and how to fix it: --- ### How many bad tries? How long locked out? The exact number of bad attempts and lockout duration are controlled by your institution's Active Directory policy, not by the SMB/CIFS server itself. The documentation doesn't specify the exact threshold or duration, but typical AD policies lock out after **5–10 bad attempts** for **15–30 minutes** (or until an admin resets it). You'll need to check with your IT/identity provider or contact FASRC support for the exact policy. --- ### What you're likely doing wrong The most common cause is entering the **wrong username format** in the Map Network Drive dialog. Windows may cache a wrong credential and replay it automatically on every login attempt, triggering repeated failed logins. **Correct format for Cannon home directory mapping:** - **Folder path:** `\\cannon.fas.harvard.edu\home\your_username` - **Username:** `rc\your_username` (with the `rc\` prefix — this tells Windows to authenticate against the FASRC domain, not your local machine) - **Password:** Your FASRC password **Key things to double-check:** 1. **Use `rc\your_username`** — not just `your_username`, and not `your_username@fas.harvard.edu`. The `rc\` prefix is critical. 2. **Uncheck "Reconnect at sign-in"** if you're troubleshooting — this prevents Windows from automatically retrying with cached bad credentials. 3. **Check "Connect using different credentials"** so Windows prompts you fresh each time instead of using a cached (possibly wrong) credential. 4. **Clear old credentials** from Windows Credential Manager before retrying: - Open **Credential Manager** → **Windows Credentials** → remove any entries for `cannon.fas.harvard.edu` or `\\cannon.fas.harvard.edu`. --- ### Recommended steps 1. Clear any cached credentials for Cannon from Windows Credential Manager. 2. Open **Map Network Drive** again. 3. Enter the folder as `\\cannon.fas.harvard.edu\home\your_username`. 4. Check **"Connect using different credentials"**. 5. When prompted for username, enter `rc\your_username` (replace `your_username` with your actual FASRC username). 6. Enter your FASRC password. 7. **Do NOT check "Reconnect at sign-in"** until you've confirmed it works. If you continue to get locked out, contact FASRC support to have your account unlocked and to verify your AD credentials are working properly.
✅ Expected Answer
You are almost certainly letting Windows authenticate with your local PC account. In the Map Network Drive dialog you must tick "Connect using different credentials"; if you don't, Windows tries your local PC information and after three failed attempts you are locked out. The lockout expires automatically in about 5 minutes. When prompted for network credentials, prepend your FASRC username with RC\ (for example RC\jharvard) so you authenticate against the RC domain.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Question 83: question_83

❓ Question

I'm on a collaborator's network where I can't run a second VPN client. Can I still get files off the cluster with FileZilla, or is the VPN mandatory for everything?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-client-update, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/license-server, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Good news: **you do not need a VPN to use FileZilla** for SFTP transfers. The FASRC documentation explicitly states that you can connect to the cluster via SFTP using the public-facing login hostnames: * **Cannon:** `login.rc.fas.harvard.edu` * **FASSE:** `fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` These are accessed over standard SFTP (port 22), so you can use FileZilla from any network — including a collaborator's — without running a second VPN client. ### Quick setup in FileZilla 1. Open **Site Manager** → **New Site**. 2. **Host:** `login.rc.fas.harvard.edu` (or `fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` for FASSE). 3. **Protocol:** *SFTP – SSH File Transfer Protocol*. 4. **Login Type:** *Interactive* (this is crucial — it will prompt you for your password and OpenAuth token). 5. **User:** your RC username. 6. Under the **Transfer** tab, check *Limit number of simultaneous connections* and set it to **1** to avoid repeated token prompts. 7. Click **Connect** and enter your password and OpenAuth token when prompted. ### A couple of caveats * **OpenAuth token:** You'll need your OpenAuth token (set up via the [OpenAuth setup process](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openauth/) if you haven't already). The token is time-limited, so if your session drops, you may need to re-enter it. * **Firewall restrictions:** If the collaborator's network blocks outbound port 22 (SSH/SFTP), you won't be able to connect. In that case, you'd need an alternative (e.g., `rsync` over a different port, or asking the collaborator to open port 22). * **Large transfers:** For very large data sets, consider using `rsync` or `globus` instead, as they handle interruptions better than SFTP clients. If you run into connection issues, let me know what error you see and I can help troubleshoot.
✅ Expected Answer
You can still use SFTP. Mounting cluster storage as a drive does require the VPN — cluster storage must be routed through a VPN connection — but SFTP to a login node does not require a FASRC/FAS VPN connection, because login nodes use two-factor authentication. FASRC recommends FileZilla as a cross-platform SFTP client; you need OpenAuth set up plus cluster access and a home directory.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Question 84: question_84

❓ Question

My home dir is full and now rm won't even work — it says 'No space left on device' when I try to delete something. How am I supposed to clean up if I can't delete anything?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_nompi.cu, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a known edge case: when your home directory is over quota, the filesystem won't let you create new blocks, so even `rm` (which needs to allocate a block to update the directory) can fail with **"No space left on device."** ### Workaround: Truncate files to zero bytes first You can free up quota space without creating new blocks by **truncating** large files to 0 bytes. Once you're back under quota, normal `rm` will work again. **Option 1 — `truncate`:** ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/bigfile.out ``` You can truncate multiple files at once: ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/file1 ~/path/to/file2 ~/path/to/file3 ``` **Option 2 — redirect `/dev/null`:** ```bash cat /dev/null > ~/path/to/bigfile ``` ### How to find what's using space Once you can run commands again: ```bash cd ~ du -h --max-depth=1 . ``` Repeat deeper into subdirectories to pinpoint large files. ### If `du` and `df` disagree If `df -h ~` shows you're over quota but `du` shows less, you may have **sparse files**. Use: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` to see the logical (apparent) size. ### Other common space hogs - **`~/.local`** — grows when you `pip install` outside of virtual environments. You can rename it temporarily: `mv ~/.local ~/.local.bak` (make sure no jobs are running under your profile first). Reference: [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/)
✅ Expected Answer
Shrink a file to zero bytes instead of unlinking it. Pick a large file you intend to remove and run `truncate -s 0 FILENAME` (you can list several files: `truncate -s 0 FILE1 FILE2 FILE3`), or equivalently redirect the empty device into it with `cat /dev/null > ~/mybig.file`. Once enough space is recovered to get you back under quota, `rm` will work again. Use full paths when deleting to avoid removing the wrong file.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
This is a known edge case: when your home directory is over quota, the filesystem won't let you create new blocks, so even `rm` (which needs to allocate a block to update the directory) can fail with **"No space left on device."** ### Workaround: Truncate files to zero bytes first You can free up quota space without creating new blocks by **truncating** large files to 0 bytes. Once you're back under quota, normal `rm` will work again. **Option 1 — `truncate`:** ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/bigfile.out ``` You can truncate multiple files at once: ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/file1 ~/path/to/file2 ~/path/to/file3 ``` **Option 2 — redirect `/dev/null`:** ```bash cat /dev/null > ~/path/to/bigfile ``` ### How to find what's using space Once you can run commands again: ```bash cd ~ du -h --max-depth=1 . ``` Repeat deeper into subdirectories to pinpoint large files. ### If `du` and `df` disagree If `df -h ~` shows you're over quota but `du` shows less, you may have **sparse files**. Use: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` to see the logical (apparent) size. ### Other common space hogs - **`~/.local`** — grows when you `pip install` outside of virtual environments. You can rename it temporarily: `mv ~/.local ~/.local.bak` (make sure no jobs are running under your profile first). Reference: [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/)

Question 85: question_85

❓ Question

Quota says I'm at my limit but when I add up my folders with du it's way less than that. Which one is lying, and how do I find whatever is eating the space?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Neither is necessarily lying — the discrepancy is usually caused by one of two things: ### 1. Deleted but still-open files When a process has a file open and you delete it (or the process crashes), the space isn't freed until the process closes the file. `du` won't count it, but the quota system still will. Check for these with: ```bash lsof +L1 ``` This lists open files that have been deleted (link count is 0). If you find any, restart or kill the process holding them. ### 2. Sparse files Sparse files appear small to `du` (which reports disk blocks used) but are counted at their full logical size by the quota system. To see the logical (apparent) size instead: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 ~ ``` This will show you the true logical footprint and help identify the culprit. ### Quick summary - Run `lsof +L1` to find deleted-but-open files - Run `du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 ~` to check for sparse files - Once you identify the space hogs, remove or truncate them to get back under quota If you're stuck and can't delete files because the system says "no space left on device," you can truncate large files to zero bytes first to free up enough space to then delete them: ```bash truncate -s 0 filename ```
✅ Expected Answer
Neither is lying — you likely have sparse files, whose space is counted by the filesystem's quota check (what `df` reports) but not accounted for properly by `du`. To find them, show the logical size instead: `cd ~` then `du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 .` That points you at the directories responsible.

Question 86: question_86

❓ Question

openconnect on my Ubuntu box dies right after it authenticates — something about not being able to find a script to configure the tunnel. How do I fix that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common issue on Ubuntu — OpenConnect needs the **vpnc-script** to configure the tunnel interface after authentication, and it's not installed by default with the `openconnect` package. **Fix:** 1. **Install the vpnc-scripts package:** ```bash sudo apt-get install vpnc-scripts ``` 2. **Make sure the script is executable:** ```bash sudo chmod u+x /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script ``` 3. **If OpenConnect still can't find it, specify the script path explicitly:** ```bash openconnect --script /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script https://vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu/ ``` This should resolve the "script not found" error after authentication. See the full instructions at [Connecting to the FAS RC VPN from Linux clients](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn/).
✅ Expected Answer
OpenConnect needs vpnc-script. Install it if you don't have it (Ubuntu/Debian: `sudo apt-get install vpnc-scripts`; Fedora/CentOS: `sudo dnf install vpnc-script`; Arch: `sudo pacman -S vpnc`), make sure it is executable (e.g. `sudo chmod u+x /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script`), and if OpenConnect still can't find it, pass its location explicitly: `openconnect --script /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script https://vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu/` (substituting the actual path on your system).

Connecting to the FASRC VPN with openconnect on Linux, what exactly do I type at the Username prompt — just my RC login?

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
At the Username prompt, you should enter your full Harvard Key (Kerberos) username in the format `username@fas.harvard.edu` — not just your RC login. For example, if your RC login is `jdoe`, you would enter: ``` jdoe@fas.harvard.edu ``` This is the standard format for Harvard authentication services, including the VPN. The VPN uses Harvard's central authentication system, so you need your full Kerberos principal rather than just the short RC username. See the [Connecting to the FAS RC VPN from Linux clients](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn/) documentation for full setup instructions.
✅ Expected Answer
No — the username must be your RC username with the @fasrc realm appended, i.e. <rcusername>@fasrc. You then supply your RC password, and finally a six-digit authorization token (OpenAuth). If you launched openconnect with sudo, the first password prompt is your local computer password, not your RC password.

Question 88: question_88

❓ Question

I hit Ctrl-C on my srun job to stop it and it just printed some status about the tasks and kept running. How do I actually make it stop?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a known behavior with `srun`. When you press **Ctrl-C**, it sends a `SIGINT` to the `srun` command itself, but **not** to the tasks it spawned. The `srun` process may exit or print status, but the underlying job step (and its tasks) continue running. ### How to actually stop it 1. **Cancel the job step** using `scancel` with the step ID: ```bash scancel -u $USER ``` This cancels all job steps for your current job. If you want to be more targeted, you can find the step ID with `squeue -u $USER` or `sinfo`, then cancel just that step: ```bash scancel <step_id> ``` 2. **Cancel the entire job** if you want to stop everything: ```bash scancel <job_id> ``` ### Why this happens As noted in the Slurm FAQ, the `srun` command normally terminates when the standard output and error I/O from the spawned tasks end, but this doesn't necessarily happen at the same time a job step is terminated [Slurm Workload Manager - Frequently Asked Questions](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html). Ctrl-C only affects the `srun` process, not the tasks it launched. ### Prevention If you're running interactive jobs and want Ctrl-C to propagate to tasks, consider using `salloc` + `srun` in a script where you explicitly handle signals, or use `sbatch` with a script that traps signals. For interactive debugging, `salloc` followed by `srun` in the same shell session can help, but you'll still need to use `scancel` to fully terminate the step.
✅ Expected Answer
That is by design: a single <control-c> is an escape sequence that reports the state of all tasks associated with the srun command. To kill the job, press <control-c> twice within one second — that sends SIGINT to all tasks and starts a termination sequence of SIGCONT, SIGTERM and SIGKILL to all spawned tasks. A third <control-c> terminates srun itself immediately, without waiting for the remote tasks to exit or their I/O to complete. (Signal forwarding does not work at all for asynchronous steps launched with --async; use scancel --signal for those.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Question 89: question_89

❓ Question

I asked for several tasks in an interactive srun --pty session, but only one of them shows any output and the rest die instantly. What is --pty doing to the others?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The `--pty` flag tells `srun` to allocate an **interactive pseudo-terminal (PTY)** for the job step. A PTY is a single terminal session, and **stdin/stdout/stderr are attached to that one terminal**. When you launch multiple tasks with `srun --pty`, only the **first task** (rank 0) gets the terminal attached — the other tasks have no terminal to read from or write to, so they either: 1. **Die instantly** because they can't read stdin (e.g., a shell or program waiting for input), or 2. **Hang** because they're blocked waiting for input that will never come. This is a fundamental limitation: a single PTY can only be connected to one process. The other tasks launched by the same `srun` command have no terminal attached. ### What to do instead If you need to run multiple tasks interactively, you have a few options: - **Run one task per `srun`**: Launch separate `srun --pty` commands for each task (e.g., in different terminal windows or tmux/screen panes). - **Use `salloc` + `srun`**: Allocate resources once with `salloc`, then launch individual interactive `srun` commands for each task. - **Use `srun` without `--pty`** for non-interactive multi-task jobs, and redirect input/output as needed. For more details on interactive sessions, see the [Interactive Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/interactive-jobs/) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
--pty runs only task zero under a pseudo terminal. It implicitly sets --error and --output to /dev/null for every task except task zero, which can make those other tasks exit immediately — shells in particular typically exit right away in that situation. --pty also implicitly sets --unbuffered, and it applies to step allocations.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Question 90: question_90

❓ Question

I added two more #SBATCH lines further down my script, below my module load commands, and Slurm completely ignored them. I also tried #SBATCH --job-name=$MYNAME and it came out literally as $MYNAME. Why?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/OpenMP/Example5/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mathematica, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Cpp/Example6/runscript.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Cpp/Example7/runscript.sh

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
1) **Summary of Attempt**: The agent attempted to diagnose why Slurm ignored `#SBATCH` directives placed below module load commands and why variable expansion failed in job names. It performed multiple hybrid vector searches to retrieve documentation on Slurm submission script syntax and best practices. 2) **Best Possible Answer**: - **Ignored Directives**: Slurm parses `#SBATCH` directives **only** from the top of the script, immediately following the shebang (`#!/bin/bash`) line. Any directives placed after comments, code (like `module load`), or other non-directive lines are ignored. You must move all `#SBATCH` lines to the very top of your script. - **Literal `$MYNAME`**: The `sbatch` command does not perform shell variable expansion on `#SBATCH` lines. To use a variable, you must define it in the script **before** the `sbatch` call or use shell expansion syntax if supported by your specific Slurm version/config, but typically you should set the job name via the shell variable in the script body or ensure the variable is exported and the script is executed in a way that expands it (though standard practice is to hardcode or use `#SBATCH --job-name=prefix_$SLURM_JOB_ID`). However, the most common reason for literal `$MYNAME` is that Slurm reads the file as-is; it does not run a shell to expand variables in the header. If you need dynamic names, you often have to rely on Slurm's built-in `%j` (job ID) or `%A` (array ID) specifiers, or define the variable in the script body and use `sbatch` with shell expansion if invoking via a shell wrapper, but generally, `#SBATCH` lines are static text. 3) **Note**: This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50.
✅ Expected Answer
Both behaviors are documented sbatch parsing rules. Slurm stops reading #SBATCH directives once the first non-comment, non-whitespace line in the script has been reached — so any #SBATCH placed after your module load (or any other command) is never processed. And #SBATCH directives are read directly by Slurm, not by the shell, so shell-specific syntax including variable names is read as literal text, which is why $MYNAME was not expanded. Put all #SBATCH lines at the top of the script, before any commands.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

Question 91: question_91

❓ Question

I ran scontrol hold on a job that was already running because I wanted to pause it, and it just kept going. What did that command actually do to my job, and can I pause a running job at all?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
` scontrol hold` **does not pause or suspend a running job**. As the Slurm documentation explains, "attempting to hold a running job will not suspend or cancel it. But, it will set the job priority to 0 and update the job reason field, which would hold the job if it was requeued at a later time" [Slurm Workload Manager - scontrol](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html). In other words, your job continued running normally. The `hold` flag only affects the job's priority in the queue and would prevent it from being re-run if it were ever requeued (e.g., after a failure or cancellation), but it has no effect on an already-running job. **Can you pause a running job?** Slurm does not provide a built-in "pause" or "suspend" command for interactive or batch jobs in the way you might expect. However, there are a few approaches depending on your goal: 1. **If you want to temporarily stop a job's execution** (e.g., to free resources), you can send the `SIGSTOP` signal to the job's processes: ```bash scancel -s SIGSTOP <job_id> ``` To resume it later: ```bash scancel -s SIGCONT <job_id> ``` Note that this is different from Slurm's native `suspend` command, which releases the job's CPUs for allocation to other jobs. Using `SIGSTOP`/`SIGCONT` keeps the job's resources allocated but pauses its execution [Slurm Workload Manager - Gang Scheduling](https://slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html). 2. **If you want to free resources entirely**, you can cancel the job: ```bash scancel <job_id> ``` And resubmit it later. 3. **For long-running background tasks**, you can spawn a background job step that will be cleaned up when your script exits: ```bash srun -N1 -n1 sleep 999999 & ``` This is recommended for keeping a job alive while you work [Slurm Workload Manager - FAQ](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html). Let me know if you'd like more details on any of these approaches!
✅ Expected Answer
Holding a running job does not suspend or cancel it. What it does is set the job's priority to 0 and update the job's reason field, which would hold the job if it were requeued at a later time. Actually pausing a running job is the `suspend` command, and only a privileged user or an account coordinator can suspend jobs (time spent suspended does not count against the job's time limit, and a suspended job that is requeued is placed in a held state). Note also that a hold placed by an ordinary user is a user-hold, while one placed by a privileged user is an admin-hold, and the hold type determines who can `release` it.

Question 92: question_92

❓ Question

I overwrote my submit script by accident but the job is still sitting in the queue. Is there any way to get the original script back out of Slurm?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, **no** — Slurm does not store the original submit script in a way that can be retrieved after the fact. The submit script is only used at job submission time to tell Slurm what to run; once the job is queued or running, Slurm does not keep a copy of the script itself. Here's what you can try: 1. **Check your shell history** — if you ran `sbatch` recently, your command history might show the script name: ```bash history | grep sbatch ``` 2. **Check version control** — if your project is in Git or another VCS, you can recover the file: ```bash git log --oneline -- <script_name> git checkout HEAD~1 -- <script_name> ``` 3. **Check backups** — if you have any automated backups (e.g., `rsync`, `tar`, or a network drive), the file may still be there. 4. **Check the job's working directory** — if the script was in your working directory and you overwrote it, the old version might still exist as a backup (e.g., `myscript.sh.bak`, `myscript.sh~`, etc.). If none of these work, you'll need to recreate the script from memory or any notes you may have. For future reference, consider keeping a copy of your submit scripts in version control or a backup directory.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — use `scontrol write batch_script <job_id> [optional_filename]`. It writes the batch script that was submitted for that job to a file, defaulting to slurm-<job_id>.sh if you don't give a filename; pass `-` instead of a filename to write it to stdout. Only a privileged user or the owner of the job can retrieve the script.

  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Question 93: question_93

❓ Question

I want to grab a couple of nodes and sit on them for a while, but I don't want salloc to drop me into a shell that I have to keep open. Can I reserve the resources and then send work at them later from my normal terminal?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parallel-matlab-pct-dcs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can reserve nodes with `salloc` without getting an interactive shell attached. Use the `--no-shell` flag (or equivalently `--no-pty`) so `salloc` reserves the resources and returns immediately to your local terminal: ```bash salloc --no-shell -N 2 -t 01:00:00 ``` This will: - Reserve 2 nodes for 1 hour - Return you to your normal terminal immediately (no interactive shell opened) - Set the `SLURM_JOB_ID` and related environment variables in your current shell You can then submit jobs to those nodes later using `sbatch` or `srun` from your normal terminal, referencing the job ID. For example: ```bash srun --job-id=$SLURM_JOB_ID your_command ``` This approach lets you hold onto the reserved resources while doing other work in your terminal, and dispatch tasks to the allocated nodes as needed. For more details on `salloc` options, see the SLURM documentation on allocating resources interactively.
✅ Expected Answer
Use `salloc --no-shell`. It exits immediately after allocating the resources without running a command, but the Slurm job is still created and stays active, owning the allocated resources for as long as it lives — you get a Slurm job id with no associated processes or tasks. You can then run work against it by passing `--jobid=<that job id>` to srun. The job is still subject to normal constraints such as time limits, so it will eventually end on its own, or you can end it early with scancel.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html

Question 94: question_94

❓ Question

If my salloc allocation gets cancelled or hits its time limit, what signal does salloc send to the command it launched when I used -K without giving it a signal?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/power_save.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/MISC25/Slinky-KubeConEurope2025.pdf, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-key-error, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the `salloc` man page, the `-K` option specifies the signal sent to the command when the allocation ends (either by cancellation or time limit). The man page states: > **-K** signal > The signal sent to the command when the allocation ends. The signal can be specified either as a signal name (e.g., SIGTERM) or as a signal number. If not specified, the default signal is SIGTERM. So when you use `-K` without specifying a signal, the default signal sent to the launched command is **SIGTERM** (signal number 15). This means if your `salloc` allocation is cancelled or hits its time limit, the command you launched with `salloc -K <command>` will receive a SIGTERM signal, which typically causes the process to terminate gracefully. If you want to use a different signal, you can specify it explicitly, e.g., `salloc -K SIGKILL <command>` or `salloc -K 9 <command>`.
✅ Expected Answer
If you do not name a signal and Slurm is configured to signal the spawned command at job termination, the default is SIGHUP for interactive sessions and SIGTERM for non-interactive sessions. Note that -K takes its optional argument with no space (e.g. "-K1", not "-K 1").

Question 95: question_95

❓ Question

Our compute nodes have hyperthreading turned on (2 threads per core). If my job asks for 3 tasks, does Slurm hand me exactly 3 hyperthreads, and could another user's job end up sharing a core with mine?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres_share.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, here's what happens with hyperthreading and Slurm task allocation: ### Slurm allocates by **physical cores**, not by hyperthreads When you request `--ntasks=3`, Slurm gives you **3 physical cores**, each with its own dedicated hyperthread. It does **not** give you 3 hyperthreads from the same core. In other words, Slurm treats each physical core as a separate scheduling unit, and each core (with its two logical CPUs/threads) is allocated as a single unit to your job. ### Can another user's job share a core with yours? **No.** Once a physical core is allocated to your job, both of its logical CPUs (the two hyperthreads) are reserved for your job. Another user's job cannot be scheduled on the same core while your job is running. The core is exclusively yours for the duration of the job. ### Why this matters - If your job only needs 3 logical CPUs, you could request `--ntasks=3 --cpus-per-task=1` (or just `--ntasks=3`), and Slurm will give you 3 full cores. The second hyperthread on each core will sit idle unless your job explicitly uses it (e.g., via OpenMP threads). - If you want to use both hyperthreads on a core, you can request `--cpus-per-task=2` per task, or use `--ntasks-per-node` with `--cpus-per-task` to specify how many logical CPUs each task gets. ### Key takeaway Slurm's default behavior is to allocate **whole cores** (both hyperthreads) to a job. You don't get "3 hyperthreads" from 1.5 cores — you get 3 cores. This means you're not competing with other users for the same physical core, but you may be leaving half of each core's capacity unused if your workload doesn't benefit from hyperthreading. For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [SLURM job submission](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-job-submission/) and [SLURM resource allocation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-resource-allocation/).
✅ Expected Answer
With hyperthreading enabled a "CPU" in Slurm means a hyperthread (without it, a CPU is a core), but Slurm still generally allocates resources to jobs at the level of a whole core. A job requesting three tasks on a node with ThreadsPerCore=2 is therefore allocated two full cores (four threads), not three threads. Two different jobs will not share a core unless the partition's OverSubscribe configuration parameter allows it. You can check ThreadsPerCore with "scontrol show node". The exception is the atypical configuration where the admin sets SelectTypeParameters=CR_CPU with only a CPU count per node (no socket/core/thread spec) — then each thread is scheduled independently as a CPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Question 96: question_96

❓ Question

My MPI run blows up about half a minute after one rank finishes early — the rest of the ranks were still working. Can I stop Slurm from tearing the whole thing down that fast?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_mpi.cu, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclReduceScatter_mpi.cu, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise1/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/MPI_and_CUDA/mpi_cuda_2.cu, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to search the vector store for solutions regarding Slurm killing MPI jobs when one rank exits early, retrieving documentation on distributed GPU computing and MPI+CUDA examples. However, the run terminated after hitting the recursion limit of 50 without finding a specific Slurm configuration fix in the retrieved context. To answer your question: Slurm does not inherently "tear down" the job immediately upon one rank finishing; rather, the MPI library (e.g., OpenMPI, MPICH) detects that a peer has disconnected and aborts the remaining ranks to prevent hanging or data corruption. This is an MPI behavior, not a Slurm timeout. To prevent this, you need to configure your MPI runtime to ignore early exits. For **OpenMPI**, add the flag `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0` and specifically `--mca pml ob1` or use `--allow-run-as-root` if applicable, but most importantly, use the `--mca btl self,vader,tcp` or similar to ensure robustness. The key flag is often `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0` combined with ensuring your code handles `MPI_Finalize` correctly. However, the most direct way to stop the *immediate* abort is to use the `--mca pml ob1` or `--mca btl tcp,self` and potentially `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0`. A more robust solution for Slurm specifically is to ensure your MPI job script uses `--map-by` or `--bind-to` correctly, but the "blow up" is usually MPI's `MPI_Abort` triggered by a disconnected rank. You can try adding `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0` to your `mpirun` command. If you are using `srun`, you can pass MPI arguments via `--mpi=pmi2` or similar. Note: The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit 50.
✅ Expected Answer
That is srun's default behavior, not a crash: srun monitors when tasks exit and by default kills the job 30 seconds after the first task exits (producing "srun: First task exited 30s ago" followed by "srun Job Failed"). Use srun's --wait=<time> option to either lengthen the timeout period or disable the timeout altogether.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html
💬 Agent Messages (1)
🤖 Assistant Message #1
The agent attempted to search the vector store for solutions regarding Slurm killing MPI jobs when one rank exits early, retrieving documentation on distributed GPU computing and MPI+CUDA examples. However, the run terminated after hitting the recursion limit of 50 without finding a specific Slurm configuration fix in the retrieved context. To answer your question: Slurm does not inherently "tear down" the job immediately upon one rank finishing; rather, the MPI library (e.g., OpenMPI, MPICH) detects that a peer has disconnected and aborts the remaining ranks to prevent hanging or data corruption. This is an MPI behavior, not a Slurm timeout. To prevent this, you need to configure your MPI runtime to ignore early exits. For **OpenMPI**, add the flag `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0` and specifically `--mca pml ob1` or use `--allow-run-as-root` if applicable, but most importantly, use the `--mca btl self,vader,tcp` or similar to ensure robustness. The key flag is often `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0` combined with ensuring your code handles `MPI_Finalize` correctly. However, the most direct way to stop the *immediate* abort is to use the `--mca pml ob1` or `--mca btl tcp,self` and potentially `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0`. A more robust solution for Slurm specifically is to ensure your MPI job script uses `--map-by` or `--bind-to` correctly, but the "blow up" is usually MPI's `MPI_Abort` triggered by a disconnected rank. You can try adding `--mca btl_base_warn_component_failure 0` to your `mpirun` command. If you are using `srun`, you can pass MPI arguments via `--mpi=pmi2` or similar. Note: The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit 50.

Question 97: question_97

❓ Question

Why does sacct come back empty for a job I ran last Tuesday, but show it just fine as soon as I pass the job ID with -j?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, slurm.schedmd.com/federation.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/C/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise3/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This behavior is almost certainly caused by **`sacct`'s default filter**, which only shows jobs in certain states (typically `PENDING`, `RUNNING`, and `COMPLETING`). Once a job finishes and transitions to `COMPLETED` (or `FAILED`, `CANCELLED`, etc.), it disappears from the default `sacct` output. When you use `sacct -j <jobid>`, you're querying that specific job directly, bypassing the default state filter, so it shows up regardless of its final state. ### How to fix it Add `--state=COMPLETED` (or `--state=ALL`) to your `sacct` command to see finished jobs: ```bash # Show completed jobs since a certain date sacct --starttime 2024-01-01 --state=COMPLETED # Show all states (running, pending, completed, failed, etc.) sacct --state=ALL ``` You can also combine it with your username or account: ```bash sacct -u <username> --state=COMPLETED ``` This is a common SLURM gotcha—`sacct` is designed to keep the default output focused on active jobs, so you need to explicitly request completed or failed jobs.
✅ Expected Answer
sacct's default search window changes depending on which options you use. With neither --jobs nor --state specified, --starttime defaults to Midnight and --endtime defaults to Now, so a job from last week falls outside the window and nothing is returned. As soon as you specify --jobs, --starttime defaults to Epoch 0 (with --endtime still Now), so the old job is found. To list older jobs without a job ID, pass an explicit -S/--starttime. Running sacct with -v/--verbose prints the time window actually in use.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Question 98: question_98

❓ Question

sacct -X shows my completed job but every memory and CPU utilization column reads 0. Is our accounting broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Fortran/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Fortran/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
### Summary of Attempted Actions The agent attempted to diagnose why `sacct -X` displays zero values for memory and CPU utilization columns for completed jobs. It performed multiple hybrid vector searches to find documentation regarding Slurm accounting, specifically looking for reasons why utilization metrics might be missing or zero. The search results retrieved general Slurm documentation and training exercises related to job efficiency and memory usage, but did not yield a specific configuration fix for the missing data in the search snippets provided. ### Best Possible Answer Your accounting is likely **not broken**, but rather configured to not collect or display those specific metrics in the `sacct` output by default, or the job did not utilize the resources in a way that Slurm tracks as "utilized" vs "allocated." Here are the most common reasons for this behavior: 1. **`AccountingStorageEnforce` or `AccountingStorageType` Configuration**: Slurm requires specific accounting plugins to track resource usage. If `AccountingStorageType` is set to `accounting_storage/none` or similar, no data is collected. However, since you see the job, the basic accounting is on. The issue is likely that **resource usage tracking** is disabled or limited. Check your `slurm.conf` for: * `AccountingStorageEnforce=usage,energy` (or similar). * Ensure `JobAcctGatherType` is set to a plugin that collects memory/CPU data (e.g., `jobacct_gather/linux`). If this is set to `jobacct_gather/none`, you will see 0s. 2. **`sacct` Format Defaults**: The `-X` flag uses a default format that might not include utilization columns. Try explicitly requesting them: ```bash sacct -X -j <jobid> -o jobid,elapsed,ntasks,ncpus,mem,mempernode,cpu,cpu_util,maxrss ``` If these columns still show 0 or are empty, the data was not collected. 3. **Job Did Not Run or Was Killed Immediately**: If the job state is `COMPLETED` but it ran for 0 seconds or was killed before it could start executing tasks, Slurm may report 0 utilization. Check the `Elapsed` time. If it is `00:00:00`, the job never effectively ran. 4. **`seff` vs `sacct`**: Try using the `seff` command (Slurm Efficiency) for the job ID. `seff` is designed to calculate and display efficiency metrics. If `seff` also shows 0 or errors, it confirms the data is missing from the accounting database. 5. **Check `slurmdbd` Logs**: If you have access, check the `slurmdbd` logs for errors related to accounting storage or job accounting gather plugins. **Recommendation:** Run `sinfo -N` and `scontrol show config | grep -i accounting` to verify your accounting configuration. Specifically, look for `JobAcctGatherType`. If it is not set to `jobacct_gather/linux` (or your OS equivalent), that is the cause. You may need to restart `slurmctld` and `slurmdbd` after changing the configuration. ### Note on Execution This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50. The agent was unable to perform further iterative troubleshooting or tool calls to verify the specific cluster configuration.
✅ Expected Answer
Not necessarily. -X/--allocations only shows statistics relevant to the job allocation itself and does not take job steps into consideration, and without including steps the utilization statistics for the allocation are reported as zero. Drop -X (or query the steps) to see real usage. Separately, which metrics exist at all depends on the jobacct_gather plugin in use — for example jobacct_gather/cgroup with cgroup/v2 cannot provide virtual-memory metrics and shows 0 for those fields.

  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Question 99: question_99

❓ Question

Is there a way to keep an eye on just my own jobs in the queue, refreshing every 30 seconds, without typing my username or wrapping the command in a shell loop?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrontab.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/high_throughput.html, slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sched_config.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — use the built-in `watch` command with `squeue`: ```bash watch -n 30 squeue -u $USER ``` This refreshes every 30 seconds and automatically filters to your own jobs (no need to wrap it in a shell loop or hardcode your username — `$USER` expands to your login name). If you prefer a persistent terminal view without the `watch` overlay, you can also use `squeue` with the `--start` flag to see estimated start times, but `watch -n 30` is the standard way to get automatic, periodic refreshes. Let me know if you'd like help customizing the output columns or filtering by partition!
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — run `squeue --me -i 30`. The --me option is equivalent to --user=<my username>, and -i/--iterate=<seconds> makes squeue repeatedly gather and report the requested information at the interval specified (printing a time stamp with the header by default). Note the man page's PERFORMANCE warning: executing squeue sends a remote procedure call to slurmctld, so do not run squeue from loops in shell scripts or other programs, and keep calls to the minimum necessary — enough of them can degrade slurmctld and even cause a denial of service.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html

Question 100: question_100

❓ Question

I want a one-line-per-partition view of how many nodes are busy versus free, but sinfo keeps giving me several rows per partition. What do I run, and how do I read the counts it prints?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres_share.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Run **`sinfo --summarize`** (or its short form **`sinfo -s`**). ### Why this works By default, `sinfo` prints **one row per partition–state combination** (e.g. one row for `idle`, one for `alloc`, etc.), which is why you see multiple rows per partition. The `--summarize` flag collapses everything into **a single row per partition**, giving you exactly the one-line-per-partition view you want. ### How to read the output The summarized output has these columns: | Column | Meaning | |--------|---------| | **PARTITION** | Name of the partition | | **AVAIL** | State/availability of the partition (e.g. `up`) | | **TOTAL** | Total number of nodes in the partition | | **FREE** | Number of nodes that are idle/available | | **NODES** | List of node names (or a condensed form like `node[1-10]`) | ### Example ```bash $ sinfo --summarize PARTITION AVAIL TOTAL FREE NODES compute up 100 45 node[001-100] debug up 10 10 node[201-210] ``` In this example: - The `compute` partition has **100 total nodes**, **45 free** (idle), and **55 busy** (allocated). - The `debug` partition is completely free. To see only partitions you have access to, just run `sinfo --summarize` as usual. Add `-a` if you want to see hidden or restricted partitions too.
✅ Expected Answer
Run `sinfo -s` (--summarize), which lists only a partition state summary with no node state details (internally it uses the format "%#P %.5a %.10l %.16F %N"). In that output the NODES(A/I/O/T) column is the count of nodes by state in the form allocated/idle/other/total — so, for example, 2/6/0/8 means 2 allocated, 6 idle, 0 other, 8 total.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Question 101: question_101

❓ Question

Several nodes are offline and I want to see the note the admins left about why. Which sinfo option shows those notes, and how much of each note do I actually get to see?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spinal-usage-guide-for-researchers, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sview.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To see the admin notes on why nodes are offline, use the `--list-reasons` flag with `sinfo`: ```bash sinfo --list-reasons ``` This will display a table with columns like **NODE**, **STATE**, **REASON**, and **TIME**. The **REASON** column shows the note left by the admins (e.g., "Node down for maintenance" or "Hardware failure"). ### How much of the note do you see? By default, `sinfo` truncates long notes. To see the full note without truncation, add the `--long` flag: ```bash sinfo --list-reasons --long ``` This ensures you get the complete text of each admin note rather than a shortened version.
✅ Expected Answer
Use `sinfo -R` (--list-reasons). It lists the reasons nodes are in the down, drained, fail or failing state — the administrator-supplied "reason" string — but it displays only the first 20 characters of the reason field, along with the list of nodes carrying that reason. Adding -l (--long) also includes the current node state in the output.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html
