Question 102: question_102

❓ Question

I compiled my binary in my home directory on the login node and the job can't find it once it lands on a compute node. Does the scheduler copy my files out to the nodes for me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/hdf5_profile_user_guide.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the scheduler does **not** copy your files to the compute nodes for you. On FASRC, your home directory (`$HOME`) is a shared network filesystem (typically NFS or similar), so files you compile or create there **are** accessible from compute nodes—provided the compute nodes can mount that same filesystem. However, there are a few important caveats: 1. **Your working directory**: When you submit a job with `sbatch`, SLURM sets the job's working directory to wherever you submitted the job from (usually your home directory on the login node). Your compiled binary should be accessible from there on the compute node, since it's on the shared filesystem. 2. **Scratch vs. home**: If you're trying to use `/n/netscratch` or other scratch space, note that scratch is **not** automatically copied to nodes either—it's also a shared filesystem, so files written there are visible to compute nodes. But scratch is temporary and purged after 90 days [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/). 3. **Common pitfalls**: - If you compiled on a **login node** (which you shouldn't do for performance reasons), the binary should still be accessible on compute nodes since it's on the shared filesystem. - Make sure you're referencing the binary with an **absolute path** or a path relative to your job's working directory, not a path that only exists on the login node. - If you're using a module or library that was loaded on the login node, remember that **modules are not automatically loaded** in your job script—you need to `module load` them again in your `sbatch` script. **What to do:** - Use an absolute path to your binary in your job script, e.g., `/home/yourusername/mydir/mybinary`. - Ensure any required libraries or modules are loaded in your job script. - If you're still having issues, check that the file exists on the compute node by adding `ls -l /path/to/binary` to your job script. For more on job submission best practices, see [Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs to the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. Slurm does not automatically migrate executable or data files to the nodes allocated to a job — the files must already exist on local disk on those nodes or on a global file system such as NFS or Lustre. If you do want a copy on the compute nodes' local storage, use sbcast, which transfers a file to local disk on the allocated nodes using Slurm's hierarchical communications (e.g. `sbcast a.out /tmp/joe.a.out`, then run it with srun and delete it from local storage afterwards).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html
💬 Agent Messages (4)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I compiled my binary in my home directory on the login node and the job can't find it once it lands on a compute node. Does the scheduler copy my files out to the nodes for me?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: SLURM job does scheduler copy files to compute nodes home directory scratch
🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: SLURM job working directory home directory scratch files not found compute node
🤖 Assistant Message #4
No, the scheduler does **not** copy your files to the compute nodes for you. On FASRC, your home directory (`$HOME`) is a shared network filesystem (typically NFS or similar), so files you compile or create there **are** accessible from compute nodes—provided the compute nodes can mount that same filesystem. However, there are a few important caveats: 1. **Your working directory**: When you submit a job with `sbatch`, SLURM sets the job's working directory to wherever you submitted the job from (usually your home directory on the login node). Your compiled binary should be accessible from there on the compute node, since it's on the shared filesystem. 2. **Scratch vs. home**: If you're trying to use `/n/netscratch` or other scratch space, note that scratch is **not** automatically copied to nodes either—it's also a shared filesystem, so files written there are visible to compute nodes. But scratch is temporary and purged after 90 days [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/). 3. **Common pitfalls**: - If you compiled on a **login node** (which you shouldn't do for performance reasons), the binary should still be accessible on compute nodes since it's on the shared filesystem. - Make sure you're referencing the binary with an **absolute path** or a path relative to your job's working directory, not a path that only exists on the login node. - If you're using a module or library that was loaded on the login node, remember that **modules are not automatically loaded** in your job script—you need to `module load` them again in your `sbatch` script. **What to do:** - Use an absolute path to your binary in your job script, e.g., `/home/yourusername/mydir/mybinary`. - Ensure any required libraries or modules are loaded in your job script. - If you're still having issues, check that the file exists on the compute node by adding `ls -l /path/to/binary` to your job script. For more on job submission best practices, see [Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs to the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/).

Question 103: question_103

❓ Question

I ran scancel -s KILL on my job. The processes died, but the job is still sitting there holding its nodes. Why didn't it go away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, slurm.schedmd.com/troubleshoot.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is normal SLURM behavior. When you run `scancel`, SLURM sends a termination signal to the job's processes, but the job itself doesn't immediately disappear from the queue. Instead, it enters a **COMPLETING** state while it finishes cleaning up. From the SLURM FAQ: "When a job is terminating, both the job and its nodes enter the COMPLETING state" [slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html). During this state, the nodes are still technically allocated to the job until SLURM finishes its cleanup (e.g., flushing job output, releasing resources). This can take a few minutes depending on the job size and how long the processes take to fully exit. **What you can do:** - **Wait it out** — the job should transition from COMPLETING → FAILED/CANCELLED → DONE on its own, and the nodes will be released. - **Check progress** with `squeue -j <JOBID>` to see the current state. - If the job seems **stuck in COMPLETING for a very long time** (e.g., many minutes or hours), contact FASRC support — it may indicate a hung process that didn't respond to the signal. In the meantime, the nodes won't be available for other jobs until the job fully exits the COMPLETING state.
✅ Expected Answer
Because sending a signal value of KILL to an entire job cancels the job's active job steps but does not cancel the job itself, and cancelling a job step never terminates the job — the job must be cancelled to release its resource allocation. To actually cancel it, invoke scancel with no --signal option: that sends a SIGCONT to all steps to wake them up, then a SIGTERM, waits the KillWait duration defined in slurm.conf, and finally sends SIGKILL if they have not terminated.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Question 104: question_104

❓ Question

Can I wipe out all of my queued-but-not-yet-started jobs in one partition with a single command, without touching the ones already running and without listing job IDs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sched_config.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes—you can cancel all of **your** jobs that are queued (not yet started) in a specific partition with a single command, without listing job IDs or affecting running jobs: ```bash scancel -u $USER -p <partition> --state=PENDING ``` ### How it works: - **`-u $USER`** — targets only your jobs - **`-p <partition>`** — restricts to the specified partition - **`--state=PENDING`** — only matches jobs that are queued but not yet running (running jobs have state `RUNNING`, so they are excluded) This uses SLURM’s built-in filtering, so you don’t need to manually list or pipe job IDs. If you want to cancel **all** jobs in a partition (yours and others’), drop the `-u $USER` flag: ```bash scancel -p <partition> --state=PENDING ``` Note: You can only cancel your own jobs unless you have admin privileges. Let me know if you need help identifying your partition name or verifying job states first.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — filter scancel by state, owner and partition, e.g. `scancel --state=PENDING --user=bob --partition=debug` (use --me instead of --user to mean your own jobs). The -t/--state filter accepts PENDING, RUNNING or SUSPENDED, and when multiple filters are supplied only the jobs satisfying all of the filtering options are signaled, so running jobs in that partition are left alone.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Question 107: question_107

❓ Question

Where on the FASRC Cannon filesystem should I store large datasets that I don't need backed up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-cluster-storage-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For large datasets that you don't need backed up, you should use the **scratch filesystem** at `/n/netscratch`. Here's why: - **No backups**: Scratch is explicitly described as "volatile, temporary scratch space for transient data and is *not backed up*" [Scratch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). - **High performance**: It's a "large, shared temporary scratch filesystem for general use for high input/output jobs" [Scratch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). - **Generous allocation**: Each lab is allotted 50TB of scratch space [Scratch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). **Important caveat**: Files older than 90 days will be automatically deleted through a periodic purge process, which can run at any time (especially if scratch is getting full or during monthly maintenance) [Scratch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). So only store temporary or intermediate data you can afford to lose or regenerate. For reference, your **home directory** (100 GB per user) *is* regularly backed up, but it's not designed for large datasets [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage).
✅ Expected Answer
Use /n/holyscratch01/<lab>/ for fast scratch storage. It is not backed up and is periodically purged of older files.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Question 108: question_108

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed with an out-of-memory error. How do I diagnose what the actual memory peak was, and how do I request more memory for the next submission?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here’s how to diagnose the memory peak and request more memory for your next job on Cannon: ### 1. Diagnose the actual memory peak * **Use `jobstats` on a successful run:** The memory value recorded by Slurm for a job killed by `OUT_OF_MEMORY` is **inaccurate**. To get the true peak, you need a job that completes successfully. Then run: ```bash jobstats <JOBID> ``` This will show the actual memory usage. Set your next `--mem` request to something slightly larger than what `jobstats` reports, since `--mem` is a hard upper limit. * **For multi-node jobs:** `jobstats` is especially useful because it shows the memory usage on each node. Note that Slurm’s `--mem` limit applies to the maximum memory used on **any single node**. ### 2. Request more memory for your next submission Add one of the following to your `#SBATCH` directives: * **Per-node memory:** ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G # e.g., 4 GB per node ``` (You can use `M` for MB or `G` for GB.) * **Per-CPU memory (alternative):** If you prefer to specify memory per core, use `--mem-per-cpu` instead: ```bash #SBATCH --mem-per-cpu 4G ``` ### Tips * Start with a large request (job slots average ~4000 MB/core) and then use `jobstats` to refine it downward. * Requesting smaller memory amounts helps the scheduler find a node more easily. * For multi-node jobs, ensure you’re distributing tasks evenly (e.g., with `--ntasks-per-node`) so memory usage is consistent across runs. For more details, see the [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/) and [jobstats](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jobstats/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,MaxRSS,ReqMem to see the peak memory the job used (MaxRSS) versus what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script, increase the memory request via --mem=<N>G (whole-job) or --mem-per-cpu=<N>G (per-task), aiming a bit above the observed MaxRSS so future variations don't kill the job again.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 109: question_109

❓ Question

I want to run a multi-node MPI program on Cannon that uses one GPU per node. What does the SLURM submit script need to specify, and how do I launch the MPI ranks?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_mpi.cu

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's what you need for a multi-node MPI job on Cannon with **one GPU per node**: --- ## 1. SLURM Submit Script Key directives for a 2-node example: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=mpi_gpu #SBATCH --nodes=2 #SBATCH --ntasks-per-node=1 #SBATCH --gpus-per-node=1 #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=4 #SBATCH --time=01:00:00 #SBATCH --partition=gpu #SBATCH --account=<your_account> module load cuda/11.8 module load openmpi/4.1.4 # Export rendezvous info for the MPI runtime export MASTER_ADDR=$(scontrol show hostnames "$SLURM_JOB_NODELIST" | head -n 1) export MASTER_PORT=29500 export WORLD_SIZE=$SLURM_NTASKS # Launch MPI program — one rank per node, each owning one GPU srun --ntasks=$SLURM_NTASKS --ntasks-per-node=1 \ --gpus-per-task=1 \ mpirun -np $SLURM_NTASKS \ --bind-to none \ --map-by slot \ --mca btl_tcp_if_include eth0 \ ./my_mpi_program ``` ### What each directive does: | Directive | Purpose | |-----------|---------| | `--nodes=2` | Request 2 compute nodes | | `--ntasks-per-node=1` | One MPI rank per node | | `--gpus-per-node=1` | One GPU per node | | `--cpus-per-task=4` | CPU cores per MPI rank | | `--partition=gpu` | GPU partition (required for GPU nodes) | | `--gpus-per-task=1` | Ensure each MPI rank gets one GPU | --- ## 2. Launching MPI Ranks You launch with **`srun`** (preferred) or **`mpirun`** inside the SLURM job: ```bash # Option A: srun (recommended — integrates with SLURM) srun --ntasks=$SLURM_NTASKS --ntasks-per-node=1 \ --gpus-per-task=1 \ ./my_mpi_program # Option B: mpirun via MPI module mpirun -np $SLURM_NTASKS \ --bind-to none \ --map-by slot \ --mca btl_tcp_if_include eth0 \ ./my_mpi_program ``` Each MPI rank automatically inherits: - **`SLURM_PROCID`** — global rank (0, 1, …) - **`SLURM_NTASKS`** — total number of ranks (world size) - **`SLURM_NODEID`** — local node index - **`SLURM_GPUS_ON_NODE`** — GPUs available on this node --- ## 3. Inside Your MPI Program Each rank should bind to its local GPU: ```c // C/C++ example — each rank uses GPU 0 on its node int rank; MPI_Comm_rank(MPI_COMM_WORLD, &rank); cudaSetDevice(0); // Each rank gets one GPU per node ``` ```python # Python (PyTorch DDP) example import os rank = int(os.environ["SLURM_PROCID"]) gpus_per_node = int(os.environ["SLURM_GPUS_ON_NODE"]) device = rank % gpus_per_node # = 0 when 1 GPU/node torch.cuda.set_device(device) ``` --- ## Key Points - **One rank per node** (`--ntasks-per-node=1`) with **one GPU per rank** (`--gpus-per-task=1`) ensures each MPI process owns exactly one GPU. - Use **`srun`** instead of `mpirun` when possible — it integrates better with SLURM's resource management. - Make sure to load the correct **CUDA** and **MPI** modules before launching. - For GPU communication, use the **NCCL** backend (for PyTorch) or appropriate GPU-aware MPI libraries. Would you like a complete working example for a specific framework (PyTorch, TensorFlow, custom C/C++)?
✅ Expected Answer
In your submit script: choose a GPU-enabled partition (e.g. gpu), set --nodes=<N> with --ntasks-per-node=1 and --gres=gpu:1 (per-node GPU request), load the MPI and CUDA modules with module load. Launch the program with srun (not mpirun directly) so SLURM places one rank per node with the bound GPU; e.g. srun --mpi=pmix ./my_program. Use sinfo and scontrol show partition gpu to confirm node configuration before submitting.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface
